## The person

Devi Raman, 29, she/her. Made clothes for four years on a marketplace that took
14% and owned her customer list. She is moving off it and she is nervous: the
whole business is 340 orders a year and a mailing list of 1,900 people, and she
cannot afford a month of broken checkout.

She is the most technical owner in this roster — she edits her own photos, knows
what a size chart is, and will notice if a variant grid makes her enter 22 prices
by hand. She will also notice if the site is slow or ugly, because that is her
whole margin.

**What made her look:** the marketplace changed its fee structure again.

## The business

**Juniper Row** — small-batch womenswear, made in a rented studio, sold online
only. No shop, no counter.

- Everything is **size × color**: five sizes, two or three colorways
- Stock is genuinely finite — 12 of a size, then it is gone
- Two drops a year plus a permanent core range
- **Returns are 22% of orders.** Exchanges are the normal case, refunds the
  exception, and getting this wrong loses her money twice
- Ships domestically, flat rate, free over $150

## Why she is here today

1. "Move my catalogue over without typing every size twice."
2. "Run a sale without emailing 1,900 people a code that breaks."
3. "Handle a return without three messages back and forth."

## The data

### The catalogue

Sizes **XS · S · M · L · XL** throughout.

| Product                              | Colorways            | Price   | Stock per variant |
| ------------------------------------ | -------------------- | ------- | ----------------- |
| The Ash Overshirt                    | Clay · Slate · Bone  | $128.00 | 6                 |
| Sunday Trouser, wide leg             | Ink · Sand           | $110.00 | 8                 |
| Marlow Knit, merino crew             | Oat · Moss           | $96.00  | 5                 |
| The Everyday Tee, boxy               | White · Black · Clay | $42.00  | 20                |
| Linen Shirtdress with removable belt | Chalk · Indigo       | $145.00 | 4                 |

That is **5 products, 12 colorways, 60 variants**. The Ash Overshirt alone is 15
— enough to find out whether a variant grid is usable or a form to be endured.

- **New in** — Ash Overshirt, Linen Shirtdress
- **The core range** — Everyday Tee, Sunday Trouser, Marlow Knit
- **Last chance** — anything below 3 in stock (see whether this can be automatic)

### Customers to load

- **Jo Kim** — `jo.kim@example.test` — 4 orders, returned 3 times
- **Fern O'Doherty** — `fern.odoherty@example.test` — apostrophe, one order
- **Sam Ruiz** — `sam@example.test` — never bought, on the list since 2024

### The sale

`SPRING15` — 15% off, the core range only, expires in 14 days, one use per
customer, not stackable with free shipping.

## The website

**This list is the definition of done for the site** (CLAUDE.md RULE #8). Every
page real, in Devi's voice, with real photographs — no template sentence left
anywhere, and the whole thing working from the public side.

She is leaving a marketplace. The site has to do everything that marketplace did
and look better than it.

| Page                                  | What is really on it                                                   |
| ------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Home                                  | Leads with New in, one photograph doing the selling                    |
| Shop                                  | Everything, filterable, with stock states honest                       |
| New in · The core range · Last chance | Three real collection pages, not one page three times                  |
| Product pages                         | Every colorway shown, size chart where the eye goes, fabric, fit, care |
| Size guide                            | Real measurements — the single biggest cause of returns                |
| Made in the studio                    | Who makes it and where. Her whole premium                              |
| Shipping & returns                    | Flat $9, free over $150, 22% come back — say it plainly                |
| Account                               | Order history, and starting an exchange without emailing her           |
| Contact                               | A form that reaches her                                                |
| Privacy · Terms · Refund policy       | Real, published, linked                                                |
| 404                                   | Offers New in                                                          |

**Working end to end:** variant picker across 15 combinations, out-of-stock
unbuyable, cart, `SPRING15` accepted and refused for the right reasons, the
free-shipping threshold showing in the cart, reviews on product pages, newsletter
signup, and search.

**Legal.** Run `get_legal_checklist` and close it out. Privacy is always
required, and returns/shipping/refund because the shop is on. Scaffolding drafts them; **publishing is the owner's act**
in Content → Legal pages, and the footer links have to actually resolve.

**The look.** Quiet, editorial, lots of white space and big photographs. The clothes are the design.

**Also required, as on every site:** a real header and mobile drawer, a footer
carrying hours, address, socials and legal links, per-page title and description
in plain words, a social card that renders, the sitemap, a favicon, a real 404,
and the same name/address/phone everywhere it appears.

## The run

### Act 1 — Sign up, onboard, and clear the decks

Spine at speed. Then remove the apparel pack's sample catalogue — hers is
arriving and two catalogues in one shop is a mess she would not tolerate.

**Done when:** in the console, `industry = 'apparel'`, sample catalogue gone.

**Met, 2026-08-29 — late, and the second half had been recorded done while it was not.**
`industry = 'apparel'` landed at signup. The sample catalogue did not: **nine `Kestrel`
products were still on sale five days later**, including a second _The Everyday Tee_ at
$45.00 beside her own at $42.00, and a stock photo of a railway track priced as
_The Overcoat, $450.00_. Nine had been archived and nine left, and nothing said so.
Removed as Devi from **Sell › Products** — none had ever been ordered — leaving
`Showing 1-7 of 7`, her catalogue exactly. Finishing it found [325].

### Act 2 — Options before products

Set up size and color as real options once, before entering anything. If that is
not possible and every product re-declares its own sizes, that is the finding —
file it before working around it.

**Done when:** the option structure exists, or its absence is filed.

### Act 3 — The variant grid

Build the Ash Overshirt: 5 sizes × 3 colors, one price for all 15, stock 6 each.
Time it honestly. Then do the remaining four products.

Watch for: whether one price can be applied to all variants at once, whether
stock can be set in bulk, whether SKUs generate, whether the grid survives at
360px, and what happens when a colorway is removed after variants exist.

**Done when:** 60 variants exist with correct prices and stock, plus the two
accessories.

### Act 4 — Photographs and words

At least three products with real images including one per colorway, size chart
information where a customer will look for it, and descriptions in her voice —
fabric, fit, care. One description deliberately long.

**Done when:** the product pages read like a real shop's.

### Act 5 — Collections and the shop

Build the three collections, put them on the site, and make the homepage sell New
in. Set shipping: flat $9, free over $150.

**Done when:** the published shop can be browsed by collection and the shipping
rule shows in the cart at the right threshold.

### Act 6 — Be the customer, twice

Clean browser, published site, phone width for at least one of them.

1. **Buy two things** — an Ash Overshirt in M/Clay and an Everyday Tee — total
   over $150, so shipping should be free. Pay with a test card.
2. **Buy one thing under $150** and confirm shipping is charged.
3. Try to buy a variant with 0 stock. It must be impossible, not merely
   discouraged.
4. Start a third order, get to checkout, and abandon it.

**Done when:** two paid orders exist, one abandoned cart is recorded, and the
out-of-stock variant could not be bought.

### Act 7 — The return

Anneliese wants the overshirt in Slate instead of Clay.

- Take the return through the console the way Devi would
- Put the Clay one back into stock
- Send out the Slate one
- Confirm the money is right — an even exchange should move no money

Then a second case: Jo Kim wants a refund on the Tee. Refund it and confirm both
the money and the stock.

**Done when:** an exchange and a refund are both complete and the numbers are
right to the cent.

### Act 8 — The customer list

Import the 25 customers from a CSV. Then build a segment — **bought in the last
90 days** — and check the count against what you actually created.

**Done when:** the import lands, the segment count is correct and explicable.

### Act 9 — The sale

Create `SPRING15` with all four conditions. Then test it as a customer:

- On a core-range item — 15% comes off
- On the Linen Shirtdress — it must be refused, with a sentence that says why
- Twice with the same customer — the second must be refused
- After expiry (move the date if needed) — refused

**Done when:** all four behaviours confirmed.

### Act 10 — Email the list

Write a real drop announcement to the segment. Check the merge tags resolve
against real data, preview it, send it, and see what happens in dev (`email.send`
is a no-op — record the queued events, do not claim delivery).

**Done when:** the broadcast is built, previewed with real values, and its dev
behaviour recorded.

### Act 11 — Reviews and the aftermath

Leave a review as a customer on the published site, moderate it in the console,
and confirm it appears. Then check what she would look at on Monday morning: what
sold, what is nearly out, who bought.

**Done when:** a review round-trips and the reports answer those three questions.

**Met, 2026-08-26.** A review round-tripped (Tessa Wren → moderated → live on The Everyday Tee,
[255]). All three questions answer: **what sold** — Sell → How selling is going, whose Best sellers
names Marlow Knit, The Ash Overshirt and The Everyday Tee with units and revenue; **who bought** —
Sell → Orders, six named rows, and the order itself carries who bought, who sold and every payment
attempt; **what is nearly out** — Home now says it, which it would not before [258]. Nine defects
found and fixed in the act: [253]–[262].

## What only this persona proves

The **variant matrix** at a size that hurts (60 variants), collections, a
conditional discount tested against all four of its conditions, an exchange and a
refund with the stock and money both correct, a real CSV import, a segment, an
abandoned cart, and a broadcast with merge tags.

## Verification

| -------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 60 variants created without entering 60 prices by hand         | Yes, all 60, and this row was STALE — it still read "Partly, 15 of 60, four products still to build" long after the other four were built. Measured 2026-09-01: Ash Overshirt 5x3, Everyday Tee 5x3, Marlow Knit 5x2, Sunday Trouser 5x2, Linen Shirtdress 5x2 = **60 variants, none unpriced**, all created through the console on 2026-08-23. Each product carries ONE price across its matrix except the Marlow Knit, which carries two (the size uplift its $96.00-$102.00 range on the shop comes from) — so the count of prices actually typed is nearer six than sixty. The Ash Overshirt's 14 missing combinations were the first, created and priced in ONE press of **Give them all the same price**. |
| Free-shipping threshold behaves either side of $150            | Yes. One delivery option, $9.00 free over $150.00. A $128 basket is offered "Delivery · 4 days — $9.00"; a $192 basket is offered the same option "Free". Both read off the delivery step as a shopper.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Out-of-stock variant unbuyable from the public site            | Yes. XS · Bone counted to zero; the server guard held and nothing oversold. It was still SELECTABLE for the twenty seconds before the refusal, which was the defect rather than the guard — [214], fixed: a gone version renders a disabled, struck-through radio that binds nothing.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Exchange moves stock and no money; refund moves both correctly | Yes, both. Exchange: Clay 6 → 5 (sale) → 6 (return), Slate 6 → 5, order still `paid $170.00` / `refunded $0.00` — one in each direction and not a cent either way. Refund, computed by hand first: $170.00 order, $42.00 line back → $128.00 paid, $42.00 given back, `partially_paid`, one unit restocked. Matched to the cent. It took [220] and [222] to get there.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| 25 customers imported; segment count matches reality           | Yes. 29 customers after the import — 21 with a phone, 25 with tags, 25 with an address — Anneliese matched on email rather than duplicating. "Bought in the last 90 days" reads **4 of 29 match** over **Members: 4**, naming Jo Kim, Anneliese Vogt, Tessa Wren and Ravi Naidoo; the bar and the panel agree, which is the actual repair. Ten rows were refused first, for tags with a space in them.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Discount refused for the three reasons it should be            | Yes, all four, on her own shop. 1 tee $42 → "needs a basket of at least $100.00. Add $58.00 more" · 3 tees $126 → −$18.90, total $107.10 · the Linen Shirtdress added → discount still −$18.90, the dress at full price · the dress alone → "does not apply to anything in your basket" · as Rowan a second time → refused. None of the four was enforced before act 9.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Merge tags resolve against real data in preview                | Yes. The broadcast preview renders the SEND itself for the first real recipient: `{{customer.firstName}}` resolved to **Marguerite** off her own customer row, in the subject and the body, with the unsubscribe footer the send composes. No email was delivered — dev runs the console provider, which writes `accepted` and never `delivered`.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Abandoned cart recorded and findable                           | Recorded and findable: yes. The abandoned path was then checked, two days on, and **did not work** — nothing moved a basket to Walked away because nothing ever had, on any tenant ([283]). Fixed with the sweep the feature was missing, and confirmed on her screen: five under **Walked away**, `Showing 1–5 of 5`, the "Nothing here" state gone. Running it for real then exposed two more — a marked basket could not be shopped or paid for ([284], blocker) and the sweep overwrote every "Last active" with its own clock ([285]). Both fixed and confirmed.                                                                                                                                           |
| Variant grid usable at 360px                                   | Yes. 15 rows grouped by size, name left and price right, no sideways scroll; an expanded row stacks to one column with every field full width. Checked in dark and light.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |

## Standing checks

The run-wide list is in [CLAUDE.md](CLAUDE.md). These are Devi's instances of
it, and they are worked into the acts rather than saved for the end.

**Wrong moves.** Import the 25-customer CSV a second time. Refund the same Tee
line twice. Delete the Clay colorway with an open order against it. Apply
`SPRING15` to an order that has already been refunded.

**Dates.** The 14-day expiry boundary — the last hour the code works and the
first hour it does not.

**Money edge.** A partial refund of a discounted order that qualified for free
shipping: does the threshold recompute, and who ends up paying the $9?

**Buyer's side.** Anneliese's own account on the shop — her order history, and
starting the exchange herself rather than emailing Devi.

**Someone else's business.** Search for "Thistle" and deep-link a P01 product id.

## Act 82 — Telling her stock page

Her last unrated Content pane, and the one whose whole job is to talk to
software: **Tell other software**. She wants her studio's stock page to hear
when she publishes something, so she does not have to remember to tell it.

**She set one up.** Name, address, tick "Content published", Create. Except the
address she typed was `stock.juniperrow.co.uk/hooks/piggles`, the way anybody
says a web address out loud, and the app wanted `https://` on the front. What it
said about that was: **nothing.** The button greyed for an instant and the screen
did not change. The request had gone, come back 422, and the pane had rendered
the refusal 767px above the fold, measured in the page rather than guessed
([404], [405]).

**Then it saved, and lied.** A green **Active** badge, and the sentence
"Notifications are being sent to this address as events happen." Her address is
fictional. Not one message would ever arrive. That sentence is a description of
the SWITCH she set, printed where a RESULT belongs — and the table that records
every attempt, with an index built for exactly one screen, had no route and no
screen ([403]). This is the worst shape a defect takes here, because the screen
is reassuring and wrong.

**And the example address said `https://example.com/hooks/sparx`** — the other
product's name, in the box she was about to type into, in a console whose every
other word says Piggles. The check that exists to stop that scans
`wizeworks/packages` only, on the reasoning that "an app serves ONE brand and
may name it" — true of its own name, false of the other one ([406]).

**Behind a door somebody had already renamed** to "Tell other software", every
word inside still said webhook and endpoint ([408]).

**What went in.** A deliveries route and a health summary that ride on the reads
the pane already makes; a ladder of seven honest states, worst news first; a
"What has been sent" panel naming each attempt, its time, its tries and what came
back. The address is tidied to `https://` in the box on leaving the field, or
refused there with the exact edit. `components/save-failure.tsx` brings a refusal
into view and takes it away when she starts fixing it, and a codemod put it on 57
of the 119 hand-rolled copies. `check:brand` learned to read both brand trees,
JSX text, and which `productCopy` defaults are already dead — 62 findings down to
14, all of them dead behind a runtime seam, plus two live ones fixed.

**Two bugs the repairs introduced, both caught by driving rather than reading.**
`webhookState` reported "Working. 0 messages arrived." on a queued delivery — the
same defect inside its own fix. And validating the address on every keystroke
remounted the control mid-word, so typing 35 characters left one letter in the
box. Both found on screen, both fixed, both now covered by a named test.

**What she can see now.** She republished a post; the pane says **On its way**,
and underneath:

> **Still trying** · **Content published** · Sep 5, 2026, 1:40 AM · 2 tries
> We could not reach that address at all. It may be down, or the address may be
> wrong.

Which is the truth, and is what she needed on the day she typed the address
wrong.

**And now she can be told about a sale.** The picker had 35 events and not one
about an order — the single thing a clothing label most wants another system to
hear. `order.paid`, `payment.captured`, `payment.failed`, `form.submitted` and
`redirect.changed` were all published already and all missing from the list. They
are in now, under a **Selling** heading that leads it, written separately into
each console's own catalogue so neither brand depends on the other. She ticked
**Order paid** and saved ([407]). `order.placed` stays out on purpose: declared
in the registry, published by nothing, so a subscription to it would sit silent
and read as a broken endpoint.

## Act 83 — Her first review

A customer bought the Ash Overshirt, wore it three weeks, and had something to
say about it. Devi's job here is the one every maker has: read it, answer it,
and decide whether it goes on the page.

**It was written from the shop, not the console.** The product page carries a
**Write a review** button; Marguerite Oyelaran gave it four stars, a title, and
a paragraph that ends "docking a star only because the chest pockets sit a
little low for me at 5'3\"". The site said **"Thanks for your review! It'll
appear once it's approved"**, which is the truth.

**Then the console could not tell her which tab she was on.** The moderation
pane's strip read `Reviews (1)` in near-black ink on a dark pane with `Questions`
bright white beside it — and `Reviews (1)` was the tab she was on. Measured
rather than squinted at: selected `rgb(32,38,49)`, unselected `rgb(244,245,247)`,
and the sliding pill that should be behind the first one sitting there `hidden`
([409]). The first diagnosis was wrong and is written down as wrong; the real one
is that the whole strip goes dead on panes opened mid-session, arrow keys and all.
Fixed so the selected tab marks itself whenever the pill fails to draw. The
keyboard half is still broken and says so.

**And every status pill in light mode turned out to be unreadable** — On sale at
**1.77:1**, Not on sale at **2.14:1**, the Waiting-for-you badge at **1.37:1**,
against a 4.5 floor. That was [076], filed three weeks ago, correctly diagnosed,
and parked as "needs Brandon". It did not need Brandon. Fixed at 48% toward the
ink token, in both consoles, and every one now clears the floor in both themes
with the hue still visible.

**She reloaded, and the panel forgot.** F5 brought the workspace back with The
Ash Overshirt open two tabs away and the review panel saying **Choose a product
first** ([410]). The layout was persisted; the one id that told the panel what it
was for was not. It is now, beside the layout, per site.

**Then the job itself, which is good.** She replied to Marguerite about the
pockets and the Clay, pressed **Post reply** to a green toast, pressed **Publish
it** to another, and the headline moved to **4.0 from 1 published review** in
place. On the shop, the review and a **STORE RESPONSE** block are both live.

**What she could not do was take a question.** The Questions tab said "when they
do, the question appears here" and her product pages had no way for anybody to
ask ([411]). The cause was left as unknown in this file rather than guessed at,
which was the right call and also the reason it took another session: the
measurement (zero section wrappers) was right and the inference drawn from it was
wrong. The route IS `products/[handle]/page.tsx`. It takes the **silica** branch
when a product template is published, and that walker stamps no section attribute.
The gap was that **`commerce.product-questions` did not exist** — the queue, both
endpoints, the ask form and the legacy section were all built, and the path that
renders every product page had no block to place. Reviews had a registry entry;
questions did not.

**So it was built, and the whole loop ran.** Devi opened My Site → Page → Each
product, typed "questions" into Insert, and **Questions and answers** was there
under _Your shop_. It landed under Reviews; Save, Publish. Tomas Villalobos then
asked a real question from the shop — 6′2″ with long arms, what is the sleeve
length on the L, and does the canvas shrink in a warm wash — and it arrived in her
console marked **Waiting for you**. She answered it (26 inches from the shoulder
seam, preshrunk, and an offer to cut an extra inch at no charge), pressed **Show
it on the page**, and it is live under his question badged **Store**. Checked at
360px in an injected iframe: reads correctly, no sideways scroll.

**The "you may also like" half was a different fault and is [412].** The rail was
never missing. She had one, bound to `commerce.featured`, which means _the ones
the merchant tagged featured_ and nothing else — and she has tagged none, so it
correctly hid itself and her product page shipped with no cross-sell at all,
silently. Featured is the shop's window display and belongs on a home page; the
default product template asks for it anyway, which is wrong for the slot and empty
for every new shop. `productDetailPage()` now composes a **related** carousel. Her
own page was repointed by hand in the studio, the way any owner would, and her
heading is her words: **Others from the same run**, over six garments with
photographs and prices.

**And two rows in her Layers list were written in code** — `commerce.product-revi…`
and `commerce.product-que…`, among rows called "Product name" and "Shipping &
delivery", with the Inspector's identity header reading `commerce….` ([413]).
Issue [099] is the same defect under a different key. Fixed at the root this time:
`rowLabel` looks the name up in the registry rather than depending on a label
stamped at insert, so it reaches every already-stored tree with no migration, both
consoles, and the Inspector, which calls the same function. Her rows read
**Reviews and ratings** and **Questions and answers** now.

**RULE #7, twice.** Twelve panes across both consoles had their tab strips
rewritten, so Priya Nandakumar's customer record was reopened afterwards and a
real note logged about her wholesale enquiry — six overshirts, Clay and Bone, a
woven label with a week's lead time. Tab strip correct, **Note saved**, note on
the timeline. And after the label fix, her **Cart** page was opened in the studio:
its pinned core now reads **Shopping cart** with its lock, where it would have
read `commerce.cart`.

## Act 84 — The piece she saved, and the box she searches with

She has one saved piece: **Send me a message**, the contact form she built on her
Contact page and kept so she could reuse it. This act is her going to manage it —
and it took four defects to get there and back.

**She never reached it the first time.** She typed "pieces" into the box at the
top of the console, got one result under **My Site** reading **Saved pieces**,
clicked it, and the console opened a journal post called "As composed by the
founder". She had not clicked a journal post. She had clicked the only row on the
screen.

The panel is centred, so its height and the position of its top edge are the same
number. It draws small while it filters screens, then the record results land a
second later, it grows to hold them, and everything in it — the rows, and the
search field she is typing in — rises by half of whatever it grew. Measured on the
same query twice: **the field moves from y=411 to y=296, "Saved pieces" from y=500
to y=347.** Her click landed on the pixel she aimed at, about a second after that
pixel stopped being the row she wanted. Its height is pinned now, in both consoles
([414]). Re-ran the exact failing step — type, click at once, while "Looking
through your records…" was still showing — and the Saved pieces tab opened.

**Then the pane itself, which is good.** She read its reach ("On 1 page"), wrote
it a note in **What it's for** — _"The contact form. Lives at the bottom of
Contact; reuse it on Trade if we open wholesale."_ — and saved. The status bar
marked it unsaved in amber first and green after, and the note appeared in the
Saved pieces list under the name, exactly as the Details card promises.

**The card promises two places, and only one of them was true.** It says the name
and note are how she recognizes the piece "in this list and in the editor's Add
panel". In the Add panel her piece read:

    Send me a ...      Your saved pieces
    Your saved pi...

Two lines saying the same thing, neither of them hers. The note was dropped twice
in transit — once by the provider building the editor's copy of the piece without
it, once by a palette hint hard-coded to the same sentence for every piece — and
neither is a break, which is why it all reads as working ([416]). Carried through
now, live-first, with two tests.

**And the row was too narrow to read because of a second, separate thing.** Search
results carried their group in a second column on the same line, and the Insert
rail is about 215px wide. Searching "opening" returned **"Opening (wor…",
"Opening ho…"** and **"Opening over a f…"** — three names cut at the exact word
where they start to differ, so the list she chooses from could not be used to
choose ([415]). The name takes the whole row now and the group leads the second
line, where it still tells "Opening hours" from "Opening (words only)". Both fixes
land in the same row: **Send me a message / Your saved pieces · The contact …**

**One more, small.** The pane's **Where it's used** card named her Contact page,
in a row shaped exactly like every openable row in the console, and clicking it
did nothing ([417]). It opens the page now, with the app's modifier contract, and
its `color="neutral"` badge went colorless on the way past.

**Checked and NOT filed.** The list badges her piece **Site-wide** while the detail
says **On 1 page**, which reads like a contradiction. It is not: one is where the
piece MAY go (the site chrome, not only a page body), the other is where it IS.
Verified in `surfaceScopeTag` rather than assumed. Likewise the list's **Open the
editor** button, which lands on the page list rather than an editor — the pane's
empty state teaches "Open the editor, build something, select it, and choose Save
as a piece", so it is a step name, not a wrong destination.

**RULE #7.** The launcher fix touches every pane, so after it a real job was done
somewhere else with it: opened her Contact page from **Where it's used**, searched
Insert for a block by name, and read three full names back.

## Act 85 — The screen that blamed her account

**What people searched for** is the last screen in Get Found she had not opened.
For her it has exactly one state, because the Google connection is not available
on this platform, so the whole screen is about how it says no.

It said no like this:

> **Not available yet**
> This connection is not switched on **for your account** yet.

No button. Nowhere to go. And the noun is false. Whether this works is decided by
two environment variables and a key on the server —

```ts
env.GOOGLE_OAUTH_CLIENT_ID && env.GOOGLE_OAUTH_CLIENT_SECRET && isTokenCryptoConfigured();
```

— one answer for every tenant at once. Her account is not consulted, and neither
is her plan. So the sentence does not merely fail to help: **it names the wrong
thing and sends her to work on it.** Settings, then plan, then billing, then
somebody's inbox, asking for a switch nobody can flip. It appears twice more on
**How people find you**, each time under an **About Search Console** button that
leads back here — three statements and one loop — one day before her trial ends,
beside a banner asking her to set up payment ([418]).

All three now say whose side it is on and that there is nothing for her to switch
on or ask for, and this screen carries a **What you can see today** card with a
button through to the one that works. The other screen is named from the registry,
not typed, so it reads "How people find you" here and "Search performance" in
sparx.

**And it wore two names.** The nav row and the launcher row both call it **What
people searched for**; the tab said **Search Console**. The registry is the single
place a screen is named and its own comment says so — "one lookup renames a screen
in all seven places at once, and there is no seventh place for one of them to be
missed". The pane walked around it, re-asserting the platform's title with
`ctx.setTitle` on every mount ([419]). That call exists for a pane naming a
RECORD, not for a pane re-typing its own static title. Deleted in both consoles;
the tab now reads what the nav reads.

**Then the job the screen exists for, done next door.** **How people find you**
was already rated and was re-checked in light and at 360px this time. Opened her
worst-scoring page from Recently checked — `/book`, 62, "Needs work" — read
**Fix this first** ("A very short title wastes the best chance you have of being
found"), pressed **Edit this page**, and wrote the two things it asked for:

> **Title in search results** — Book a fitting at the Juniper Row studio
> **Description in search results** — Choose a time to come in for a fitting or an
> alteration. The studio is at 1418 Larimer Street in Denver and a visit takes
> about half an hour.

Saved, published, came back. Still 62 — until Refresh, then **62 → 79**, "Needs
work" → "Good", Title and summary 57% → 100%, Being found at all 84% → 100%, and
**Fix this first** moved on to the next real thing. The site average went 76 → 77.
That confirms this pane's standing gap (2) as measured rather than asserted: the
score is right, it just waits to be asked.

**Checked and NOT filed.** The page-check measures the page NAME as the search
title when no search title is set, and the editor's Name field says "Visitors
never see this" — which looks like a contradiction. It is not: the search title
field's own helper says "Leave it empty and the page name is used", so both
sentences are true and the check is measuring what a search engine really sees.
Read the field before filing.

## Act 86 — Two green badges that meant two different things

The last unrated screen. **Translations** lists her eight products and what each
one is written in; a row opens that product's translation editor.

**The editor is the best one in this module, and this time the whole loop was
driven, not just the form.** She finished The Ash Overshirt's French — its name
was already "La surchemise Ash", its description still English — wrote the
paragraph, and saved. Then she opened her own shop as a French reader would:

    /products/the-ash-overshirt?lang=fr
    → "La surchemise Ash", her French paragraph,
      browser tab "La surchemise Ash · Juniper Row"

and at the foot of the site, a language strip: **American English · Español ·
Français**. Clicking **Español** on the same product gave English back, because
there is no Spanish on it — the fallback the pane promises, working. [401] is
confirmed from a shopper's seat rather than from an endpoint.

**Then the list, whose old rating said "Nothing to fix: it is a truthful index".**
It was not truthful in the one column it exists for.

Three products carried a green badge — Marlow Knit (Spanish), The Ash Overshirt
(French), The Everyday Tee (Spanish) — and one of the three was nothing like the
other two. Proved by doing what a busy owner does: added Spanish to The Everyday
Tee, typed only the name, saved. Same green badge as the two that are finished.

The name is the **only** field a translation cannot be saved without — the editor
says so under the box. So a row existing means the name is done and nothing else,
and the badge was built from the row existing:

```ts
byProduct.set(id, result.data.map((row) => row.locale).sort(…));
```

One `.map` to the tag, and the description, both search fields and the row's own
date all thrown away. The ordinary case is the bad one: type eight names in one
sitting, come back to eight green badges, conclude the shop is in Spanish, and
have every shopper meet an English paragraph under a Spanish title ([420]).
Green now means a reader gets the product in their language; **amber**, with
_"Spanish has no description yet"_ under it, means they get its name and then your
own words. Nothing new was fetched — the same query, read properly.

**Two more in the same column** ([421]). A column headed **Changed**, next to
**Languages**, on a screen called Translations, was the PRODUCT's date: a Spanish
translation saved on Sep 5 sat beside **Aug 28**, and the column was filled in for
five products never translated at all. It reads **Last translated** now, from the
translation rows, with an em-dash where there are none. And at 360px the table
scrolled sideways — **346px of table in a 323px box**, measured, with the
**Languages** header clipped to "Language" — because the identity cell was capped
at a flat 288px, wider than the whole pane. That is [390] again on a list that
never adopted the constant written to end it. It uses `IDENTITY_CELL` now, and the
status badge and the date ride under the name rather than vanishing.

**Checked and NOT filed.** A French reader gets French product words inside an
English shop — "Choose yours", "Add to cart", "Low stock" are all still English.
That is the feature's scope (it translates products, and says so), not a defect in
it. Worth knowing, not worth filing as this pane's fault.

## Act 87 — The half of a defect that had already been fixed

[409] carried the only open code thread left in this run: its pill was repaired,
its **keyboard was not**. "Roving focus is still dead on those strips … it needs
the upstream integration looked at."

**It was not dead.** It was measured before the redundant `ScrollStrip` was
deleted and never measured again after, and that deletion — recorded at the foot
of the issue as an afterthought — was the fix.

Driven as Devi, by keyboard alone, on two strips opened MID-SESSION, the state
that reproduced the failure:

- **Reviews & questions** — clicked **Reviews (1)**, pressed **→** (focus moved to
  Questions), pressed **Enter**. The panel switched, Tomas's question and her
  answer appeared, and the fill moved with it.
- **The Ash Overshirt** — clicked **Overview**, pressed **→** three times,
  **Enter**. The Media panel opened with her photograph in it.

Measured after:

    Overview:ti-1 | Options:ti-1 | Variants:ti-1 | Media:ti0* | Details:ti-1 | …
    indicator hidden: false   width: 69px   selected background: rgba(0,0,0,0)

Exactly one tab at `tabindex="0"` and it is the selected one — that IS roving
focus. The earlier reading, "every tab lands on `tabindex="-1"`", was a registry
that never populated, and it populates now. The CSS fallback correctly paints
nothing. The issue is corrected in place rather than tidied, because inheriting a
note's diagnosis is exactly what cost the time.

**And looking up a customer to test it found a live one.** Typing **Priya** — a
customer's name — and pressing Enter opened the **Content** list, because the
highlighted first row was **Privacy Policy**, two edits from what she typed. Three
customers actually called Priya sat below it. Reproduced immediately on a second
name: **Marguerite** put the opening line of a review first and the woman who
wrote it second.

Only one half of the panel was ranked. Surfaces went through a careful ladder;
records were appended **in the search server's order**, and that order is
typo-tolerant by design — right for finding, wrong for deciding what Enter opens
([422]). With a mouse it costs a second; with the keyboard, which this project
checks deliberately and which the panel advertises in its own footer, it opens the
wrong pane with nothing to undo.

Records are ranked now, on their own ladder: the row's name, then the line under
it, then the server's order for anything the client cannot see a reason for.
Nothing is dropped, because typo tolerance is the point of the server's
generosity. "Priya" leads with three Priyas and Enter opens her record;
"Marguerite" leads with Marguerite, then her four orders, then her review — the
person, then what she did, then what she said.

## Act 88 — The other console had no tests, and four fixed defects were still in it

Not a persona act: Brandon asked that **sparx fit the same tests**, with the one
standing constraint that neither brand tree imports the other.

`piggles/apps/workbench` has a test seat and its config says why an app earns
one — the console decides what a person READS, and those rules are pure functions
whose failure is invisible to typecheck, lint and the eye. `sparx/apps/workbench`
had **no seat at all**: no config, no `test` script, no vitest. So the same rules,
in the same shapes, went unchecked there.

It has one now, and porting the tests found **four defects already fixed here and
still live there** ([423]). Not one was a decision anybody made; each was a fix
that landed in one console and never crossed.

- **Setup would rebuild a business that was already running.** [364]'s guard is
  piggles-only. sparx registers both setup flows as ordinary surfaces — its own
  comment says "setup is never a one-way door" — with nothing in front of them,
  and the first commit sends the whole module switchboard, lays a ready-made site
  over the real one, and renames the business. It has a `SetupGate` now.
- **Every unmeasured file said it weighed nothing.** [380] again, in three places:
  sparx coerced a missing size to zero and printed **0 bytes**.
- **A shop with sales was told no page had sold anything.** `salesUntraced` is
  piggles-only; sparx rendered `0 (0%)` down every row when nothing could be
  traced, with no caveat.
- **A failed write blamed a connection that was never broken.** [386] again:
  `!navigator.onLine` is true when the browser says nothing at all, and a 503 was
  still folded into "you are offline".

**44 tests pass in sparx**, 119 here, both consoles clean on tsc and lint, all
thirteen structural checks green — `check:boundaries` included, and grepped both
ways: no import crosses between the trees.

Eight of the thirteen piggles test files cover modules sparx does not have
(billing lifecycle, blueprints, forms, redirects, webhooks), so there was nothing
to port. The two consoles stay COPIES rather than a shared package, because that
is the rule; `check:console-parity` keeps them in step, and the test seat is now
the second thing that does — the one that catches a fix landing in only one.

## Act 89 — The other console had the parts and had never been given them

Not a persona act either: Brandon asked for the sparx-piggles parity list to be
worked and closed. `check:console-parity` compares by NAME, and says so in its
own header, so the list it produced under-reported every item on it.

Four items, and only one of them was the size its file count suggested
([424]).

- **`<PaneLoadError>` was built in sparx and never adopted** — 2 call sites
  against 200 here. Three hundred surfaces had each invented a failure state,
  and the invented ones said the wrong thing: a DELETED record read "the server
  could not be reached", over a **Try again** that can never succeed. It is 98
  call sites now, and the reason is read from the failure rather than assumed.
- **`lib/api-error.ts` swallowed every `VALIDATION_ERROR`** — so a service
  explaining a real business rule arrived as "Check what you entered."
- **307 toolbars still passed their contents as `children`**, and a slot the bar
  cannot name is a slot it cannot fold, so narrow panes spilled onto extra rows.
- **`inventory.costing.uncosted` did not exist there.** sparx warned that stock
  had no cost recorded and offered nowhere to fix it. The API route was already
  shared; only the screen was missing.

Two more turned up on the way. **⇧-click was silently broken**: chrome has no
pane to name as the anchor, `positionFor` reads that as "no opinion", and so
every ⇧↵ from the launcher opened a plain tab under a footer advertising "⇧↵
alongside". And **five `ctx.open` calls pointed at nothing** — typos parked in
`KNOWN_DEAD_OPENS` behind a note about waiting for somebody who owns sparx. Five
buttons that opened nothing. The allowlist is empty now.

**Windows mode was not a gap.** `lib/window-mode.ts` said in its own header that
this is Piggles chrome and sparx is not offered it, backed by 22 recorded parity
exceptions. Brandon reversed that on 2026-09-05 — "we have proven its worth in
piggles" — so it was ported and the false header was rewritten in BOTH consoles.
Tiling is still what sparx opens on. Saved arrangements now carry `mode` and
`zoom`, because a grid is pixel boxes measured at whatever zoom was on screen.

`check:console-parity` reports **0 divergent**; sparx's excused `lib` files went
27 to 10 and its reachable modules 154 to 174. Both consoles clean on tsc and
lint, 44 tests there and 119 here, all thirteen structural checks green.

**The lesson is recorded, because it nearly cost more than the work.** A cleanup
regex in the toolbar sweep spanned the content between two comments and silently
deleted the filter chips from seven lists, the create button from three, and a
saved-views bar. Typecheck, lint, prettier and 44 tests were green through all
of it, because deleted JSX has no callers. A sweep is finished when its own diff
has been read back, not when it compiles.

## Act 90 — The screen said she was taking payments. Nothing was being charged

Back in her seat, and back to the thing she actually said she was afraid of:
"I cannot afford a month of broken checkout." So before anything else, does
checkout take money?

She searched **payment** in the box at the top. Good results, and correctly
grouped: **How you take payment** under Sell, separate from **Payments** under
Money. She took the Sell one.

The pane opened and its first sentence was right:

> You're set up with Manual payments. You take the money yourself — checkout
> places the order and nothing is charged online.

Six lines under that, on the Manual payments row, a **green** badge:
**Active — taking payments**. And above the whole pane, a second green badge:
**Taking payments**. Opening Manual payments said it a third time — "Checkout
uses Manual payments to take payments."

Three green claims that money is coming in, and three plain sentences in the
same eyeful saying nothing is charged. The green is what gets scanned; the
sentence is what gets read after something has gone wrong. For a shop with no
counter and 340 orders a year, believing the badge means every order arrives
unpaid and she hears it from customers rather than from here ([425]).

The fact needed to tell the two apart was already on the descriptor and already
being served — `checkout: 'none'` — it simply was not read. `gatewayState` is
the one place the surface takes status from, so the fix landed there and the
row, the pane header and the detail heading all followed. Green now means money
is moving and is the only thing that means it.

**While in the file** (root RULE #4): the same function returned `neutral` for
two different things, so **Available** and **Coming soon** were the same grey
pill. `tone` is optional now — absent means a colorless badge, which is the
right ink for a row carrying no state — and Coming soon takes `info`, because a
gateway you cannot pick yet is a state and not an absence.

Then she opened **Your own Stripe**, which is where she is actually going, and
the screen asked her to "Paste these from your **Your own Stripe** account"
([426]). Eight sentences did it, including the confirm that removes her keys.
The shelf name has a real job — it has to tell that entry apart from the
platform's own gateway — but it is not the name of the company whose dashboard
she opens in the other tab. The descriptor carries `processor` now; Stripe is
"Stripe", and the one gateway whose processor is unknowable by definition says
"your payment processor account".

**What could not be proved.** Entering a live secret key into a field is not
something I may do, so Save keys, the webhook section and "Make this my active
provider" were read and not driven. Recorded rather than claimed.

`gatewayState` and `checkoutSummary` are pure functions that decide what a
person reads about money, which is exactly the shape act 88 gave the consoles a
test seat for. Nine tests now cover them in both — including one asserting that
a gateway with no online checkout never carries the words "taking payments", and
one asserting that active-and-charging never shares a tone with
active-and-charging-nothing. **128 tests here, 53 in sparx**, both consoles clean
on tsc and lint.

**One thing noticed and not chased.** The trial banner across the top says "Set
up payment to keep your site online", and its button is about paying Piggles.
It sits four inches above a pane called "How you take payment", which is about
her customers paying her. Two unrelated things wearing the same word, stacked.
Recorded in Open threads rather than filed — nothing is broken, and whether it
is worth renaming is a judgement about the trial banner, not about this pane.

## Act 91 — Coats cost more to send, and the shop had no way to say so

Her delivery rules were already right: US only, $9 flat, free over $150. So the
next ordinary thing for somebody selling coats and scarves out of one studio:
**coats cost more to send.**

The screen says she can do that, in as many words — "Add another only if some
products ship differently… **so they can be priced on their own**." She made
**Coats and heavy knits**, went back to her US domestic region, and the delivery
option form grew a control that had been hidden while she had only one group:
**Which products it applies to.** Coat delivery, $25.

Then she looked at her own checkout, with a scarf, a shirt and a knit in the
basket — **nothing in the coats group** — and was offered:

> Delivery · 4 days — Free
> **Coat delivery — $25.00**

**Delivery groups were ignored completely.** `rateShipment` walked every rate in
every matching zone and never looked at which group a rate belonged to. Every
group's price was offered to every basket ([427], blocker). Wrong money in both
directions: a $25 surcharge on somebody buying a scarf, and — the direction that
costs HER — a cheap small-parcel rate would have been offered on every order in
the shop, discovered from the postage bill.

Then the half that is worse. **There was no way to put a product in a group at
all.** Not on the product's seven tabs, not anywhere in the console, and no API
route either: `assignProductsToProfile` sits in the commerce service and nothing
has ever called it. The group screen's own promise — "You add products to the
group from each product later" — had no later. The feature shipped
three-quarters built: create a group, price a group, then either leak that price
onto everybody or leave the group empty forever.

Two more on the same screens: the list called **any** empty group "All other
products", so a brand-new group claimed the whole catalog; and the group's own
pane said "No products yet" about the very group the list called "All other
products". One of them had to be wrong.

**The rule now lives in one tested module** (`shipping-profile-match.ts`, 20
tests), because it decides money: an item resolves to ONE group; the shop's
**oldest** group is the fallback; an option is offered only if its group is in
the basket; and a mixed basket is priced by the **dearer** group, because one
parcel has to satisfy the strictest thing in it. That last one is a decision
rather than a discovery — the other answer is splitting the order into a parcel
per group, which is a different feature — so it takes the direction that never
under-charges her.

**Putting a product in a group is now a thing that exists.** `shippingProfileId`
on the product PATCH, at most one per product, and a control under
**Details › How this one is delivered** that hides itself when the shop has only
its standard group. And `defaultProfileId()` is one function, read by the rate
matcher and by both screens, so they cannot disagree about which group is the
default.

**The rule was wrong once, and the screen is what caught it.** The first version
made the fallback "the group with no members" — correct for a shop with one
group, and wrong the second she made another, because then there are TWO empty
ones. It made Coats the fallback for a basket of scarves and priced the scarves
as coats: the same defect turned around. Twenty tests were green while that was
true. Age is unambiguous and does not move under her.

**Proved twice, as the shopper.** A basket of scarf + shirt + knit with nothing
in a group is offered "Delivery · 4 days — Free" and nothing else. Then, as
Devi, the Marlow Knit went into **Coats and heavy knits** and saved; the same
basket is now offered **"Coat delivery — $25.00"** and nothing else. The list
reads "Coats and heavy knits — 1 product" and "US domestic — standard goods —
All other products", and the group's own pane agrees. Dark, light, and 356px.

**153 tests in commerce**, 128 here, 53 in sparx; commerce, commerce-schemas,
api-rest and both consoles clean on tsc and lint.

## Act 92 — Her shop charged no sales tax, on a screen that said it was collecting

**Sell › Tax** is one of the better screens in the console. No jargon: "A place
is somewhere you are registered to collect tax — usually because you have a
shop, an office, staff, or enough sales there." Four places listed, three with a
rate and a green **Collecting** badge.

Two things were wrong, and one of them made the other real.

**First: nothing has ever charged tax.** A customer in San Francisco filled her
basket — shirt, knit, scarf, $634 — and the summary read Subtotal $634.00,
Shipping $25.00, **Total $659.00**. No tax line. Not $0.00; nothing.
`taxService.calculate` had **no callers anywhere in the repo** — not checkout,
not the cart, not B2B, not the market quote. The only mention of it outside its
own file is a Prisma comment saying it is "enforced at checkout" ([428],
blocker).

Measured three ways before touching anything: zero callers; no tax line on a
California basket; and in the database **every order taken through the real
checkout carries zero tax** — 17 of them across three persona shops. The only
rows with tax are older demo tenants where the seed wrote a figure in directly.

Two more found on the way in. `calculate` parses `customerExemptionIds` and
never reads them, so a reseller with a certificate on file paid like a walk-in.
And an address carries free text in State / Region — "CA", "California" — while
a tax place is filed as `US-CA`, with nothing translating between them; that one
would have made the fix look like it worked while quietly charging nobody.

Tax is now priced where shipping is priced, in `submitShipping`, for the three
reasons already written at that spot: its own reads, never inside the write
transaction, never from the client. It swaps in the total the way shipping does,
so changing the address cannot stack charges. `taxRegionCode` translates the two
vocabularies and says **nothing** rather than guessing — an unrecognised region
falls back to the country place, which under-charges visibly instead of charging
a stranger the wrong state's rate. Certificates are honoured, and
`coveringExemption` hands back WHICH one applied, because an order has to record
that or the shop cannot answer an auditor. 18 new tests.

**Then: her Denver studio was described as having staff in three states.**
California, New York and Texas, each reading "**You have a presence here**",
each **Collecting**. She never typed any of them — they were created 28 minutes
after signup by the **clothing store** starter, which installs three zones at
`nexusType: 'physical'`, `isActive: true` ([429], major). The starter did say it
included "US sales tax"; that is not the same sentence as "you are registered in
California, Texas and New York and are now charging there."

It had been harmless only because nothing charged tax. **Fixing the first made
the second live**: the same afternoon, a California basket went from no tax line
to **$45.97**, taken under a claim she never made. Every tax pack now installs
**switched off**, says so in its own description, and the trap is written into
the file. A place that is off no longer states a reason for being there —
"Nothing is charged here yet" instead of asserting a presence.

**Proved both directions on her own checkout.** To San Francisco, with
"California" typed as a NAME: Subtotal $634.00, Shipping $25.00, **Tax $45.97**,
Total $704.97 — 7.25% of the goods, shipping untaxed, which is exactly what
California's rate says. Back to Portland: $659.00 and **no tax line**, and
switching back REMOVED the tax rather than leaving it stacked.

**Two things named rather than done**, both because they are questions for her
or for Brandon rather than answers I get to pick: she still has no **Colorado**
place, so she collects nothing where she certainly does have nexus; and existing
tenants are **not backfilled** — every shop created from these starters still
has three states switched on and, since this act, is charging in them. The
migration is easy; "turn off tax collection for every existing shop" is not a
change to land without him seeing it.

**171 tests in commerce**, 128 here, 53 in sparx; commerce, commerce-schemas,
api-rest and both consoles clean on tsc and lint.

## Act 93 — She could sell a gift card, and nobody could ever spend it

**Sell › Gift cards** says what it is for in her own words. The empty state: "A
gift card lets someone pre-pay an amount **for another person to spend with
you**." The issue form: "A unique code is created for you. Share it with the
recipient so they **can spend it at checkout**."

So I issued one — $150 for Nadia Ashcombe, with a birthday message. Back came
**QM44-2DTN-6HM4-6RA9**, balance $150.00, and a green badge: **Ready to spend**.

Then I went to her shop, filled a basket, and typed the code into the only code
box on the page.

> **No active discount for code "QM44-2DTN-6HM4-6RA9"**

**A gift card could not be spent anywhere** ([430], blocker). `applyGiftCardToCart`
had zero callers in the repo. `redeemGiftCard`, the function that takes money off
a card, had zero callers. There was no API route to spend one, no box at
checkout, and `appliedGiftCardCodes` on a basket was hardcoded to an empty list.
The word "gift" did not appear on the checkout page at all. The money went one
way: she could load a card, hand over the code, and carry the liability while the
customer had no way on earth to use it.

Third time today. A finished, well-worded screen sitting on a write path nothing
ever ran — the same shape as the tax that never charged and the delivery groups
that priced nothing.

**One box now takes either kind of code.** A shopper holds a code; she does not
know whether the shop filed it as a discount or as a card, and should not have
to. The basket says **"Discount or gift card code"**, and the server tries the
discount first and then the cards — that order on purpose, because an unknown
discount code costs the shopper nothing while reserving the wrong card takes
money off someone's balance. Applying RESERVES; the balance only moves when the
order is written, so an abandoned basket leaves the card whole and there is no
reversal to get wrong.

**A card is money already paid, not a discount, so it is booked as a payment.**
The order's total stays the value of what was sold; what came off the card is an
`OrderPayment` with the code as its reference, which is what makes "Still owed"
right. Putting it in the discount total instead would understate her revenue and
the tax base under it.

Three things were quietly wrong inside the dormant code and all three are fixed:
the reservation capped against RAW line prices and so ignored every discount on
the basket; applying a card never re-added the basket up, so the price would not
have moved on screen; and a checkout session never re-read the card from its
basket, so a code applied after checkout began would have shown in the cart and
vanished at the till.

**And the code was on no screen.** Piggles showed it nowhere — the toast that
announced it faded, the browser tab truncated it, and the pane she opens to give
a customer their code did not contain it. It is now the identity heading, in
mono, with a copy button beside it. sparx already had the heading and gains the
button.

**Proved end to end, twice.** A $40 card typed in LOWER CASE against a $58 scarf:
Gift card −$40.00, a chip with the code and an ×, total $18.00; through checkout,
$9.00 postage, **$27.00 to pay**. Afterwards the card reads **balance 0, spent**,
with `+15000 issue` then `-4000 redeem` against the order in its own ledger, and
order **O-000016** carries total $67.00, paid $40.00, **partially_paid**. Her
order pane reads "**$27.00 still owed** — $40.00 of $67.00 has come in so far",
and under **Money in**: "**$40.00 · Gift card** · 969G-HVUS-BCAT-7PW2 · Taken".

That run also settled a question left over from act 91: a scarf on its own was
offered **Delivery · 4 days, $9.00**, not the $25 Coat delivery. The delivery
group rule prices a mixed basket by the dearer group and leaves a plain one
alone, which is what it was written to do.

**One thing named rather than built.** A gift card cannot be taken at the
COUNTER. Record payment offers Cash, Check and Wire transfer, and adding "Gift
card" as another way there would be worse than the gap — it would write money in
against an order without taking anything off a card. Doing it properly needs the
code, a balance check and the debit, exactly as the basket now does; that is a
capability to add rather than a defect to patch.

**182 tests in commerce**, 128 here; commerce, commerce-schemas, crm-schemas,
api-rest, the storefront and both consoles clean on tsc and lint.

## Act 94 — Only a person switches tax on

Not a persona run. Brandon read the open decision from act 92 and did not
recognise the question:

> regarding the taxes, I don't know what you are talking about. my question is,
> how should this be handled correctly? that is how we should solve it.

Which was the right answer to a badly framed choice. Act 92 had left four
options about whether to switch existing shops' tax zones off, on the grounds
that changing live merchant settings was a deploy decision. That framing assumed
there were merchant settings to change. **There were not**, and asking the
database took one query:

```
 country | region | is_active | registration_number | never_touched | count
---------+--------+-----------+---------------------+---------------+-------
 US      | US-CA  | t         |                     | t             |     9
 US      | US-NY  | t         |                     | t             |    10
 US      | US-TX  | t         |                     | t             |     9
```

Every switched-on tax place on the platform has no permit number, no
registration date, and `created_at = updated_at` — **not one has ever been
opened by a human**. Twenty-eight machine-written rows and no merchant decision
among them. There was no trade-off to weigh; I had offered a choice about
protecting something that did not exist.

**The correct handling is one sentence: a machine may set tax up, only a person
may switch it on.** `is_active` could not express it, because it held both "the
owner decided this" and "something set this up" in one boolean, and something
had been writing them. So the facts are now two columns — `activated_at` records
the moment a signed-in person switched collection on — and the rule is enforced
four times over: the input schema defaults to off, the service refuses to CREATE
a collecting place at all (the shape of the request is the test, not who signed
it, since a starter installs under the new owner's own session), a database CHECK
refuses an unclaimed active row outright, and `calculate` matches only zones that
carry the record. One
exported `zoneIsCollecting` is that rule, and both consoles draw their badge
from it, so no screen can say "Collecting" about a place that takes nothing.

The backfill needed no permission either, and the reason holds on every database
rather than on the one I counted: `calculate` had no callers until [428], so no
shop here has ever charged a cent of sales tax. Switching every unclaimed place
off cannot interrupt collection that never happened. Nothing is lost but the
switch — place, rate, nexus type and permit number all stay. Written as "has
nobody claimed this?" so it states the rule and a re-run leaves alone a place a
person has since turned on.

Devi is told why, in her own words, and only when it means something to her: a
place that carries a rate and charges nothing gets "**Set up, but charging
nothing** — Tax is worked out and added at checkout. Every place starts switched
off, so nothing is charged before you have looked at it." A brand-new shop, whose
one seeded place has no rate, is told nothing, because there is nothing wrong.

Two things fell out of the same rule. **A new tax place now starts switched
off** — the line above that form always claimed nothing was charged until you
switched it on, and the switch was pre-set to on. And **the demo seed writes a
permit number and a registration date** on the places it activates, because a
demo shop trading for a year is registered where it collects, and the CHECK now
refuses the alternative.

Proved rather than assumed: the migration was run inside a transaction and rolled
back. It switched 28 places off, left 64 all off, and then **refused an attempt
to switch one back on without a person behind it**. The dev database came out of
it byte for byte unchanged.

Brandon then said to run the handoff commands. `prisma generate` went through and
the last outstanding error cleared: **459 tests in commerce-schemas (7 new), 182
in commerce, and a full api-rest run of 85 files / 492 tests all green**, both
consoles clean on tsc and lint, with console parity, surface routes and
boundaries green. That full api-rest run also settles the loose end from act 93,
where a run reported `83 passed (84)` with `Errors 2`: it does not reproduce.

`prisma migrate deploy` was refused twice by the tool's safety classifier and
then allowed on a second pass. **Applied: 28 places switched off, 64 all off,
`activated_at` and `tax_zones_active_needs_a_person` live.**

Then driven as her, with the shop up. **Sell › Tax** opens on "Set up, but
charging nothing" over four silent places, none of them claiming a presence any
more. **Add a place → Colorado** offers no collect switch at all; rate set to
2.9%; switched on, at which point the line under the switch reads "**Charging
here since September 6, 2026**" and the row goes green. `US-CO is_active t,
activated_at 2026-09-06 07:35:55+00`; CA / NY / TX still off with no stamp.

Three baskets, the same $128.00 overshirt with $9.00 delivery:

| Ship to          | The place                         | Charged                 |
| ---------------- | --------------------------------- | ----------------------- |
| Portland, Oregon | none exists                       | $137.00, no tax line    |
| Denver, Colorado | on, she switched it on, 2.9%      | **$140.71 — tax $3.71** |
| California       | 7.25% rate, **nobody claimed it** | **$137.00, no tax**     |

The California basket is the whole point: the rate is sitting there and nothing
was taken, where yesterday it would have been **$9.28** for a state she has never
dealt with. Dark, light and 360px all check out, no horizontal overflow.

**One defect found while confirming, and fixed.** The DETAIL pane still asserted
"You have a presence here" on a place switched OFF — the same defect as this act
is about, one screen along from the list row that was fixed. Both consoles now
read "Nothing is charged here yet" until the place actually collects.

Folded into [429](issues/429-her-denver-studio-was-told-it-had-staff-in-california-texas-and-new-york.md),
which now carries the whole design; no new issue and no rating change, since no
new pane was scored.

## Act 95 — A template could have renamed her business

Chasing the oldest open thread: her public payload said the business was
**"Saffron & Sage Catering"**. The note guessed another shop's name had leaked
across the wall between tenants.

**It had not**, and the check was two queries: no tenant on the platform carries
that name, and the string is in exactly ONE row — the `businessName` key inside
her primary site's own brand override, sitting beside her own real tagline. So
the guess was wrong, which is the ordinary outcome of a note written without a
measurement ([[feedback_verify_capability_in_code_not_docs]]). Chasing it anyway
was still right, because of what was underneath.

The name came from `marketplace-catalog/blueprints/sparx-catering-events`, and
the INSTALLER already refuses to carry it — issue [210] decided that a template
gives you a look while the name and the words are the merchant's. **The UPDATER,
its twin, never got the rule.** It read `businessName` and `tagline` out of the
override, merged them and wrote them back; and the merge resolves an automatic
change by always taking THEIRS. So a blueprint whose next version changed its
sample name would have **renamed her business** on "The design your site was
built from has been refreshed" — silently, no conflict shown. That banner was on
her Home screen while this was being read. Third time in two days that a rule was
right on one path and missing on its twin: the tax preset and its bootstrap, the
tax list row and its detail pane, and now this.

Then the second half, found only by trying to answer "who wrote this?":

```
select action, count(*) from audit_logs where entity_type='Property' group by 1;
 builder.site.published | 126
 builder.site.reset     |   2
```

**Every settings change and every publish on her site was logged. Nothing about
the site record itself.** `/v1/properties` wrote no audit entries at all, so a
site could be created, renamed, re-branded, promoted, or DELETED with no record
of who or what it had been called — and deleting one takes its pages, layouts,
forms and addresses with it.

Both fixed, and both filed as [431](issues/431-a-design-update-could-rename-her-business-and-nothing-would-say-who-did.md).
Proved by creating a site called **Audit Check** through her own Settings › Sites
and deleting it: `tenant.site.created` then `tenant.site.deleted`, each carrying
the name and slug, the delete entry outliving the row it names. Site count back
to seven.

Her stale value is left alone and said so: it is inert everywhere it was checked,
and a sanctioned script already exists whose stated job is to strip the dead key.
Running a backfill is a pipeline decision. Named rather than built: **no screen
can edit or clear that key** — which is how it sat unnoticed for two weeks — and
the right direction is finishing its removal, not giving a deprecated key a UI.

api-rest clean on tsc, lint and 176 tests.

## Act 96 — Half-finished checkouts, finally looked at

The pane rewritten before the crash and never seen. All of it works: **Not
finished** leads the filters and is the default, and above the table the sentence
she opens this list for — **"9 checkouts, worth $2,202.00."** Switching to All
re-states it as 22 and $4,382.30, so the number always describes what is actually
in front of her. Filtering to Expired gives the empty state its own words rather
than a blank drawer.

Two things only looking could have found.

**At 360px the stage column drops**, so a phone showed who and how much but not
where the payment stuck — on a pane that exists to say exactly that. The badge
now rides under the shopper below `@xl`, and the wide layout is untouched.

**The shopper was a raw email**, which the earlier score had already called out.
The fix was not a join: **`customerName` is a column on the session itself**,
written at the till beside the email, and simply never selected. Every other list
of people in the console shows a person; this one showed an address because one
line of a `select` was missing. Another of
[[feedback_fetched_but_never_rendered]] — the data was in the table, one field
away.

Rated **Design 7 → 8, Ease 6 → 9** in [rating.md](rating.md). Not a 10: from this
list she can see a stalled checkout but cannot reach the shopper about it, which
is a capability rather than a defect.

Clean on tsc, lint and console parity in both consoles.

## Act 97 — A customer bought something and heard nothing back

Nine orders sitting **To send** and unpaid, three past their due date. The order
pane is good — it says "$180.00 still owed · No money has come in for this order
yet", it has a Money in box to write down a payment that arrived, a delivery
block, returns, cancel. What it does not have is any way to **ask** for the
money. Looking for one found something worse.

**Nothing on this platform has ever sent an order confirmation.**

Measured, not inferred. The `order-confirmation` Builder email is provisioned,
published and live on her shop — one of thirty. Zero automations trigger on
`order.placed`, on her shop or on ANY shop (`select count(*) … where
trigger_type='order.placed'` → 0). No seeded automation names the template.
`checkoutService.complete()` sends no mail. And the only mention of it in the
automation code is the comment above the four order emails that DO exist, calling
them "the counterparts to order-confirmation that were missing" — someone built
the counterparts believing the original was handled. Fourth instance in three
days of [[feedback_screen_over_a_function_nobody_calls]], and the first where the
artifact with no caller was a finished, published EMAIL.

It bites hardest on a shop like hers. Her checkout says: "Placing this order does
not take any money now… **We'll be in touch about paying for it.**" The shopper
presses **Place order — $140.71 to pay** and receives nothing. No number, no
summary, no sign of the promised contact. On a shop that takes money at the till
a confirmation is a courtesy; on a pay-later shop it IS the handover. Two of her
seeded automations make it worse by being unfirable: **High-value order alert**
waits on `order.paid` and **Payment failed — email** on `order.payment_failed`,
and her shop takes no payment at checkout, so neither can ever run. The one event
she produces every single time had nothing listening.

Fixed with one seeded automation mirroring the four that exist — `order.placed` →
the `order-confirmation` email, transactional, guarded on `customer.email`, no
delay. Everything it needed was already there: `order.placed` is in `ORDER_EVENTS`
so the customer and order refs resolve. **Existing shops need no migration**:
`reconcileSystemSeeds` runs daily off the automation worker and idempotently
upserts the catalog for every tenant whose module is active. That is the
mechanism the tax fix had to be argued through by hand.

**And a test that would have re-broken the notification bell.** `notify.test.ts`
asserted `entityType` is `'Order'`; the seed writes `'order'`. Lowercase is
load-bearing — it is the key `routeForEntity` looks up in `@wizeworks/links`, and
all 28 entity keys there are lowercase. `'Order'` resolves to nothing, which is
the dead end that file's own comments say was fixed. Corrected, with the reason
beside it.

Filed as [432](issues/432-a-customer-bought-something-and-heard-nothing-back.md).

**Named rather than built: she still cannot ask a customer for money.**
`BillingDocument` carries `customerId` and `companyId` and no `orderId`, so an
invoice cannot be tied to an order and paying one would not settle it. That is a
capability with schema, email and a shopper-facing surface behind it. The
confirmation is the first half of the promise her checkout makes; the second half
is unbuilt, and saying so is better than pretending the gap is an oversight.

## Act 98 — She marks an order sent, and the customer is never told

Act 97 fixed **one** email. The rule it came from says to sweep the set, so I
asked the database which of the 43 provisioned templates any automation actually
names. Eleven had nothing. Six of those eleven turned out fine — the five
`booking-*` are sent by the scheduling notification ledger and `waitlist-offer` by
the offer path, neither of which is an automation. That left five with no sender
anywhere, and one of them was the next thing Devi was about to need.

**`shipping-confirmation` has never been sent either.** `order.fulfilled` does
fire — `order-fulfillments-service.ts` publishes it the moment a parcel is marked
shipped or handed over, and **55 automations listen**. Every one is the
post-purchase review request. So the shopper's sequence was: buy (silence, until
432), Devi marks it sent (**silence**), then three days later _"How did we do?"_
about a parcel nobody ever told them was coming.

**And underneath it, the same twin-path shape a third time.** The template
declares `refs: ['customerId','orderId','fulfillmentId']` and the event carries
`fulfillmentId` in its payload, but the resolver for `order.fulfilled` was
`hydrateOrder(orderId)`, which drops the rest of the payload, and
`entityRefsFromFields` had no `fulfillmentId` line. The render survives that only
