by guessing: `resolveShipping` falls back to "the latest fulfillment on this
order", so an order shipped in two boxes sends the first box's email carrying the
second box's tracking number.

**A third, found on the way out.** The same function was dropping `bookingId`.
The console offers "Somebody books an appointment" as a trigger and the resolver
does hydrate the booking, so conditions work — but every booking email source
reads `ref.bookingId` with no fallback. An owner's own "when someone books, email
them" would have sent a confirmation with the date, the time, the service and the
place all blank.

Three edits, each copying a pattern already in the file: the two parcel events get
their own resolver group that merges `fulfillment.id` on top of the hydrated order
(exactly as `SUBSCRIPTION_LINK_EVENTS` does), `entityRefsFromFields` gains both
refs, and a `COMMERCE_SHIPPING_CONFIRMATION_EMAIL` seed sits between the
confirmation and the delivered notice so the catalog reads in the order the
customer lives it. No migration — the daily reconcile again.

The test ships **two** parcels and fires the event for the second, then asserts
BOTH sends leave. That is deliberate: the review request always did fire here, and
something going out is exactly how the missing one stayed hidden.

Filed as [433](issues/433-she-marks-an-order-sent-and-the-customer-is-never-told.md).

**Left standing, deliberately.** Three `appointment-*` templates sit published on
7 shops with nothing able to send them — the previous generation of the five
working `booking-*`, referenced by no code anywhere. Juniper Row is not one of the
7, and clearing them means deleting rows on other people's tenants, which is not
mine to do unasked.

## Act 99 — Doing the job, and what doing it showed

Dev came back, so I did Devi's actual work: sent one of her nine waiting orders.
Three defects on the way, and the middle one is the only proof that act 98
actually worked.

**The list broke her order numbers in half.** First screen of the working day, in
a docked pane: `O-` over `000016`, and "Due Thu, Sep 10" over four lines with the
last one clipped, so the date a late job was late by was the part that
disappeared. The Order column had 83px; the Delivery column, holding a small
badge, had 118. Nothing stopped either line wrapping, and auto table layout will
always break a token before it takes space from a neighbor.

Stopping the wrap exposed what the wrap had hidden: **six columns do not fit.**
719px of table in a 676px pane, and what fell off the right edge was the Total —
"$67" where "$67.00" belongs. The disclosure ladder is by container width and
otherwise well judged; its top rung was just set too low, letting the sixth
column in at 672. Placed was already the last to appear, so it now waits for
`@3xl`. Ten rows visible where five fit before. Then at 360px, 26px of sideways
scroll traced to `truncate` with nothing to truncate against — a name has no
natural width, so with no cap it simply grew the column. That is the failure
`IDENTITY_CELL`'s own note describes, on a list that never adopted it. Filed as
[434](issues/434-the-order-number-broke-in-half-and-the-money-fell-off-the-edge.md).

**Then I sent the order, and act 98 held up.** Marked O-000013 sent by USPS with
a tracking number. The toast says "The customer can follow it from here" — a
sentence that was simply false a day ago. The run completed, and the queued mail
carries `entity_refs.fulfillmentId` = the exact parcel I had just created, with
`fulfillment.id` in the snapshot beside it. Not the test passing: the real flow,
her tenant, her customer.

**And the mail named the courier "usps".** The template binds
`{{shipping.carrier}}` and the resolver returned the stored column raw, so the
one place this fact left the business was the only one not translating it. The
translation existed three times over and had already drifted: the consoles said
"Sent by the supplier" where her own website said "Drop-ship", the website had no
entry for `other` so its `toUpperCase()` fallback showed a shopper the word
"OTHER", and a parcel with no carrier put the literal word "Carrier" on her order
page. One `carrierLabel()` in `@wizeworks/commerce-schemas` now; three local maps
deleted. An unknown code is returned unchanged rather than shouted. Filed as
[435](issues/435-the-email-told-her-customer-the-parcel-went-by-usps.md).

**One thing needs the user.** `wizeworks/apps/site` had no dependency on
commerce-schemas. It is added and the import is in place, but the package is not
linked yet, so that one file needs a `pnpm install` before it typechecks. Not
mine to run mid-session.

## Act 100 — Her queue is empty, and clearing it found four more

Nine orders were waiting when this run started. **There are none now.** Seven
went out this act, one of them a collection, and every despatch with an emailable
customer produced a shipping confirmation carrying the right parcel.

**She could not add a tracking number after the parcel went.** The toast on
"Mark it as sent" said, always, "The customer can follow it from here." The
tracking field is optional and leaving it blank is the normal case for a shop
that walks its parcels to the post office — and with no number the customer's
email has no tracking row and no "Track your package" button at all. So the
sentence was false. I changed it to say which of the two happened, wrote "Add a
tracking number later if you get one", and then checked whether THAT was true.

It was not. A shipment was frozen the moment it was made. `PATCH
/v1/orders/:id/fulfillments/:fulfillmentId` has always existed, the service
behind it has always existed, and **nothing in either console called it** — only
GET and POST appear anywhere in the two apps. Which is the same gap, on the same
block, that somebody already fixed for the other half: the comment above
`useRecordFulfillment` says the POST "has always existed and nothing in either
console called it". The create half was wired. The update half was left.

Built: `useUpdateTracking` in both consoles, and an inline editor on each
shipment row reading "Add a tracking number" or "Change the tracking number". An
empty value sends `null`, so a number typed by mistake can be removed. A
collection gets no form. And the audit log, which recorded status ONLY, so an
edit that set a tracking number wrote a diff saying nothing had changed — it now
carries the number on both sides, because "who changed it, and from what" is
exactly the question asked when a parcel goes missing. Filed as
[436](issues/436-the-parcel-went-and-the-tracking-number-had-nowhere-to-go.md).

**And a defect I put there myself in act 98.** The fourth order in the queue was
a collection. The pane handles that well — "The customer is coming to fetch this
one", one button, no carrier field. But a collection is recorded as a fulfillment
carried by `pickup` and publishes `order.fulfilled` exactly like a despatch,
deliberately, so the activity feed and the review request see the sale complete.
My shipping confirmation listened to that event with one condition,
`customer.email is_set`. A customer who had just walked out of the shop holding
their order would have been emailed "Your order is on its way — track your
package". Ravi has no email on file, so nothing went. That is luck, not a guard.

Fixed with `fulfillment.carrier neq pickup`, which first meant making the field
resolvable at all: the act-98 resolver merged only `fulfillment.id`, and the
payload carries ids alone. It now reads the ROW and exposes carrier, service,
tracking number and status. **The guard was checked both ways** — with the
condition removed the new test fails, so it is load-bearing rather than
decorative. Filed as
[437](issues/437-a-customer-who-collected-in-person-would-be-told-their-order-had-shipped.md).

**Two smaller ones, in passing.** A real carrier service name rendered as "USPS ·
USPS Ground Advantage Economy"; when the service already names the carrier it now
says it once. And the tracking input had no placeholder and took the whole row,
pushing Save onto a line of its own.

**Seen and deliberately NOT fixed.** Two of the eight despatches produced no mail:
their runs are recorded `failed` with "no executor registered for action
email.send_campaign". The cause here is almost certainly dev hot reload — the
action registry is a module-level Map and editing that package mid-session
re-evaluates it empty. But the tail is real: `failRun` marks the run failed,
bumps a counter, and that is all. **No retry, no alert** — the comment beside it
says pause-on-repeated-failure is "a later (UI) slice". So a transient failure
loses a customer's order confirmation permanently and the only trace is a counter
on a screen a clothes maker will never open. Retry policy, backoff, dead-lettering
and who gets told are a capability with real decisions in them, not a patch.

## Act 101 — Asking for the money

Her queue of unsent orders was empty. What that left was the other half of every
sale: **$1,756.50 owed across eight orders whose goods have already gone**, plus
$214 part paid. Her checkout promises "we'll be in touch about paying for it", so
being in touch is the job.

I drove it rather than reading it. There IS an Invoices screen and a well-built
New invoice form — document type, customer typeahead, due date, addresses, line
items, notes, summary. **Nothing on it knows orders exist.** To chase $234.60 she
had to open the order, read the items, come back, find the customer again, retype
every line by hand, and type a tax rate as a number on a shop that already has tax
places and permit numbers set up. And when the customer paid, the ORDER still read
"Not paid · $234.60 still owed" for ever, because no column joined the two.

`BillingDocument` carried `customerId` and `companyId` and no order. Its one
order-shaped link, `convertedOrder`, points the OTHER way: "this quote became that
order".

Brandon asked for it directly — "she should send an invoice" — which settles the
question I had twice parked about whether this was a capability or a defect.

Built: `billing_documents.order_id`; a `createInvoiceForOrder` that copies every
line, the delivery charge, the frozen addresses and the order's own tax; the
already-received money carried across as a deposit so the balance is what is truly
owed; **the settlement hook**, so paying the invoice writes an `OrderPayment` and
moves the order to part paid or paid; `GET`/`POST /v1/orders/:id/invoices`; and an
"Asking for payment" section on the order pane in both consoles.

Three decisions that carry weight. It **copies the sale and never re-prices it** —
the order is the record of what was agreed, and an invoice charging a different
number would be a second opinion about a sale that already happened. The seeded
deposit is written **straight to the payment table rather than through
`recordPayment`**, which is load-bearing: `recordPayment` is where the settlement
mirror lives, so routing the seed through it would count the same dollars twice.
And a **refund is not mirrored back** — refunds are their own lifecycle, and a
negative capture would corrupt the rollup both paths share.

It refuses by name in the three cases where an invoice would be wrong, and the
console hides the button in those states rather than offering a control whose only
job is to fail.

The migration was dry-run in a rolled-back transaction: DDL clean, the foreign key
accepted 88 real links and refused an order that does not exist, dev database
unchanged. Both consoles typecheck; an eight-case integration test is written.

Filed as [438](issues/438-she-shipped-the-goods-and-had-no-way-to-ask-for-the-money.md).

## Act 102 — Sending it

Brandon authorized the migration, so act 101 could finish. Finishing it was where
most of the value was, which is the lesson of the act.

**Three of the eight tests failed on the first real run.** One was my test being
wrong: it read payment rows through the bare Prisma client, and every billing
table is row-level-secured, so it saw nothing — and would have "proved" its point
by finding nothing at all. A test that can only pass by seeing rows must read
through the service.

The other two were one real defect, and a bad one. **`createInvoiceForOrder`
skipped `applyStageEntryEffects`** — the shared helper whose own comment says it
exists so "the initial stage is treated identically to any later transition", and
which is what MINTS THE NUMBER. Two paths create a billing document and only one
ran the rule. An invoice raised from an order arrived with no number, no due date
and no frozen letterhead: outside every aging bucket, impossible to chase, and
`sendInvoice` refuses an unnumbered document by design. **The capability I built
could not have done the thing it was built for.** Same shape as the rule I keep
writing down — a rule right on one path and missing on its twin — except this
time the missing twin was mine, written the same day.

Then the browser found the second one, which nothing else could have. The first
invoice raised opened with a **blank Billing name and a blank Email**, under a
label reading "Where the invoice gets sent". Commerce freezes a structured
address (`recipientName`, `line1`, `region`, `postalCode`); an invoice's `billTo`
is three flat strings, `{ name, email, address }`. I had copied one into the
other. It type-checks, it saves, and it silently empties the invoice — the Bill
to form, the "billed to" column, the PDF and the greeting on the emailed invoice
all read the three strings and find none of them.

**Then I read my own screen and found three sentences that were not true.** The
button said **Send an invoice** and only made one. The section said "the invoices
you have **sent**". And each row printed **"Sent 7 Sep"** off the date the
document was _created_ — telling an owner her customer had a bill that had never
left the building. The row now carries the real `sentAt` from the send route, or
says "Not sent yet · open it to email it".

Sending it for real found two more, on opposite ends of the same path. The
Piggles Send dialog resolved the recipient itself from `billTo.email`, which is
blank on most invoices, so it announced **"There is no email address on this
invoice yet"** about an invoice whose customer has one — and then offered a
**Send it** button that works, because the server knew the fallback and the
screen did not. And **the sparx console cannot send an invoice at all**: its two
outbound actions both end by handing the job back to the operator's own mail
client. Both fixed; filed as [439](issues/439-the-send-button-said-there-was-no-address-and-sent-it-anyway.md).

Then the database was asked the wider question, the way act 98 asked it. Nine
invoice automations are live on her tenant and **not one fires on
`crm.billing_document.created`**. Seven invoice-shaped email templates exist and
none of them is "here is your invoice". So the platform will email her customer a
friendly reminder three days before a bill they have never seen is due, then
overdue, then a second notice, then a final notice. Four escalating chase letters
for a document that never went out. Filed as
[440](issues/440-four-automations-chase-an-invoice-nothing-ever-sends.md) with
the reason it is not just a seed: `service-repair` has two payable stages and
there is no dedupe at the send layer, so the naive rule sends twice.

One more, found on the way past. `billing_documents.issued_by` is filled in on
every finalized invoice, with a long comment about how renaming a site otherwise
rewrites the letterhead on invoices already in customers' hands. **Nothing reads
it** — not the snapshot payload, not the PDF, not the email, all of which go to
the live site name. Filed as
[441](issues/441-the-letterhead-is-frozen-on-every-invoice-and-printed-on-none.md).

**Proven as Devi, end to end.** O-000013, fulfilled, $180 owed: Make an invoice →
INV-000002 numbered and addressed; deadline of Sep 21 typed and saved; Send →
`email_events` recorded `accepted` for anneliese.vogt@example.com; the order pane
now reads "Sent Sep 7, 2026 to anneliese.vogt@example.com · Due Sep 21, 2026";
payment recorded against the invoice → **order O-000013 flipped to `paid`**, with
an order payment naming INV-000002. That last step is the whole point of the
column.

INV-000001, raised by the broken code before the fix, was repaired by hand rather
than deleted.

**What this act is really about.** Every one of these was found by continuing
past the point where the work looked done. The migration applied, the tests were
written, both consoles typechecked — and the feature still could not have sent an
invoice, because the number was never minted. Typecheck, lint and a green test
file all passed over a capability that did not work. Only running it did.

## Act 103 — Not chasing a bill nobody sent

[440](issues/440-four-automations-chase-an-invoice-nothing-ever-sends.md), closed.
The interesting part is what did NOT get built.

The obvious fix is an automation that emails the invoice the moment it is
raised. Using the screen says otherwise. **An invoice is an edited document** —
the create flow opens the editor, and the first things an owner does are set a
deadline and check a line. Auto-sending on create emails a document she has not
finished, which turns "edit an invoice after it has gone out" from a wrong move
into the normal path. The double-send hazard is the second reason and it stands:
`service-repair` has two payable stages and there is no dedupe at the send layer.

So the shape is: **the platform never sends a bill on her behalf, and never
chases one it did not send.** Every dunning step now requires `invoice.sentAt`,
which meant making that field resolvable — off the metadata bag, because there
is no column and the send route is its only writer.

**The guard has a cost that had to be paid for in the same act.** An invoice she
never sends is now never chased AND never sent, silently — which is exactly
[[never present absence as measurement]] waiting to happen. So the other half is
making an unsent bill visible where she looks for money: every unpaid row on
Invoices carries a **Not sent** badge beside its status, and a **Sent** filter
sits next to the status one. They are different questions — status is about the
money, this is about whether the bill was ever handed over — and before this an
invoice unpaid because nobody sent it and one unpaid three weeks after it landed
read identically.

A footgun avoided on the way: the new `?sent=` parameter is `z.preprocess`, not
`z.coerce.boolean()`. The latter is `Boolean(value)`, so `?sent=false` off a
query string arrives as TRUE and the filter returns the exact opposite of what
was asked for. Checked both directions in the console rather than assuming.

**Proved red.** The two new tests drive the real shipped seed rather than a copy.
Removing `WAS_SENT` turns "does not chase an invoice that was never sent" red and
leaves "chases the same invoice once the customer has been given it" green, so
the guard cannot rot into a condition that is always true. 58 automation tests
pass. As Devi: INV-000001 ($234.60, raised in act 102 and never emailed) now
reads **Owed · Not sent**; the filter returns 1 of 1 both ways; zero overflow at
360px and 9.12:1 on the badge in dark.

**And it reaches shops that already exist**, which was Brandon's question. Both
layers self-backfill and neither needs a migration or a hand-run script: the
dunning automations are system seeds and `reconcileSystemSeeds` upserts the
catalog daily off the automation worker, and `provisionDefaultEmails` creates any
missing default email on module activation AND on a 6-hourly fleet sweep. A new
email template added to `DEFAULT_EMAIL_TEMPLATES` lands on every email-active
tenant within six hours. Blueprints carry site content, not the platform's
automation or email catalog, so nothing was owed there.

## Act 104 — The letterhead that never froze

[441](issues/441-the-letterhead-is-frozen-on-every-invoice-and-printed-on-none.md),
closed — and the title I filed it under was wrong, which is the interesting part.

I filed it as "written and never read". Asking the database corrected me: **0 of
90 billing documents carried an issuer, 52 of them finalized.** It was never
written either.

The freeze sat inside a `stageType === 'final'` block, and **the default workflow
every tenant starts on has no final stage** — its one Invoice stage is `open`. So
on the workflow almost every document uses, the freeze could not fire.

That is the same mistake, in the same function, that had already been found and
fixed for its neighbour. The due date used to sit in that identical `final`-only
block, and the comment explaining why it was moved out is still there: "the
DEFAULT workflow, whose Invoice stage is `open`, could never produce one." **One
field was moved out of the wrong block and the field beside it was left behind.**
A fix and its own next bug, shipped in one edit.

So the repair was four parts, and any one alone leaves the letterhead moving:
freeze it when the document becomes PAYABLE rather than final (the same moment
the due date is set — the two are now one rule in one block); read it when
printing; read it when emailing; and freeze it on the B2B AR ledger, which builds
its document directly and had no issuer at all. `snapshotIssuer` is exported now
so both writers call one implementation. The snapshot payload carries it too — it
froze `billTo` and `shipTo` from the start and not the seller, and a frozen
record that names the customer but not the seller is half a record.

**Proved by moving her business.** Devi's business details were empty, so her
invoices had no letterhead at all; filled in as she would — Juniper Row Textiles
LLC, 1184 SE Ash St, Portland OR 97214. Raised INV-000003 for O-000015, which
wrote the first `issued_by` this platform has ever had. Printed it: the masthead
read her name and address. Then moved the business to 2260 NW Quimby St through
the same screen an owner would use, and **printed the same invoice again — still
1184 SE Ash St, with the new address nowhere in the artifact.** Checked against
the printed bytes rather than the screen. Her real address was restored
afterwards.

Guard run red: disabling the freeze turns the new test red and leaves the other
nine green. 494 crm tests pass, plus 7 new unit tests on the decode. The existing
snapshot test caught the payload change the moment I made it, which is exactly
what a strict shape assertion is for.

Not backfilled, deliberately: INV-000001 and INV-000002 predate this and still
print from the live business. Nobody recorded who issued them at the time, and
inventing an answer now would be worse than falling back.

## Act 105 — Who owes me, and who is late

Devi had shipped sixteen orders and been paid for four. This act was her working
the other twelve, and the platform turned out to be unable to tell her the one
thing a receivables list exists to answer.

**The receivables band was written and never mounted.** `ar-summary.tsx` is a
finished, careful component — it reads `/v1/invoicing/aging` over ALL open
documents rather than summing the page on screen, precisely so it cannot
under-report past fifty invoices, and it hides itself when nothing is open. One
grep: zero importers. The invoice list even carries a note saying "it is written
to ride alongside this list, but nothing imports it today". So the pane answered
"what invoices exist", which is not why anybody opens it.

**And mounting it alone would have made it lie**, which is the more interesting
half. A due date was written only when the payer's EMPLOYER had a net-terms
window on file. Devi sells to people. So every invoice she has ever raised had
no deadline: `deriveDocumentStatus` can never call one overdue, `bucketAging`
files it under "Current" for ever, and every rung of the dunning ladder keys on a
day count off that date. Nine unpaid bills, and the band would have said **Late:
None** truthfully and uselessly, for ever.

Worse, the customer's copy already made the promise. The invoice email prints
**"Due on receipt"** when there is no date — so the platform told the customer
one thing and then recorded nothing, chased nothing and aged nothing.

The fix follows the moment the customer actually receives the bill.
`dueDateFromTerms` is one rule both writers call: the window comes from the
payer's terms, counted from a date the CALLER supplies, because the two callers
know different things. Entering a payable stage only knows when the bill was
RAISED — an invoice may sit unsent for a week — so a zero-day window waits there,
and the send route fills it in from the day it goes out. Anyone on real terms
still gets their date up front, so the deadline is on screen before she sends.
`netTermsDays` had documented "empty → 0 (due immediately)" from the start; its
only caller threw that 0 away, which is how "due immediately" became "no
deadline, ever". Its comment also still named formats the schema rejects
("net 15", "due on receipt"): the field admits `prepay` or `net`+1-365 only.

Devi is told before she clicks. The send dialog now adds "It has no deadline
yet, so it will be due when they get it" — and drops that sentence the moment
she sets one, which is the version I watched it do.

### The bill that did not add up

Billing O-000016 put four numbers on one card that could not all be true:
Total **$58.00**, Paid **$40.00**, Amount due **$27.00**, and a warning that
"$67.00 is what the customer would see today" about a document she had not
touched. [442].

`totals.ts` is the browser's mirror of the server's totals, and its own header
states the rule it mirrors — "lines → subtotal → discount → tax → **shipping →
surcharge** → total". The function under that comment stopped at tax. So the
Total row was short by the delivery charge, sat beside an Amount due read
straight off the server which was right, and the "Not saved yet" warning — the
only thing between her and emailing a stale invoice — cried wolf on every
invoice with a delivery charge. There was no Delivery row anywhere either:
`shippingTotal` and `surchargeTotal` appeared nowhere in the whole invoicing
surface.

**The customer's copy was worse, and the discount was missing too.** The emailed
summary listed subtotal, tax and already-paid. Two of the five invoices this shop
had ever sent were already in Marguerite's inbox with a hole in them: INV-000001
subtotal $276.00 asking $234.60 with a $41.40 discount invisible, INV-000003
subtotal $634.00 asking $659.00 with a $25.00 delivery charge invisible.

The printed PDF had both rows from the start. **The one artifact that got it
right is the one nobody reads on screen**, which is why every check passed. It
also needs a delivery charge to appear at all, and ten of her sixteen orders have
none — the first invoices were correct by accident.

The summary is now `invoiceSummaryRows`, a pure exported function with its own
test, and the invariant that test asserts is the one a customer applies: **the
rows have to add up to the number the bill asks for.** The template binding scope
offered subtotal, total and balance only, so a hand-built chrome block could
rebuild the same gap; it now offers tax, delivery and surcharge.

### Driven end to end

Ten invoices raised and emailed as Devi, every unpaid order billed. **$1,645.50
outstanding across nine**, on a band that did not exist this morning.

Then the part that has never been possible: INV-000001's deadline moved back a
week through her own screen, and the whole chain fired at once — the row read
**7 days late** with a Late badge and sorted first, the band turned red with
"$234.60 · 1 invoice · worst 1–30 days", and the Late filter found exactly it.
The band correctly kept counting all nine while the filter showed one, which is
what reading the aging report rather than the page on screen buys. Her real
deadline was put back afterwards.

Guards run red both ways. Removing the terms window reddens "sets the deadline
from the terms the payer agreed" and leaves the walk-in test green; always
setting it reddens the walk-in test and leaves the other four green. Dropping
shipping from the mirror reddens four of five, and the fifth — a document with
neither charge — stays green, which is exactly why this shipped.

**One suspicion checked and dropped.** The invoice email signs off "WizeWorks"
under a bill from Juniper Row, and the template argues at length against putting
a platform name on a tenant's invoice. It looked like the same neighbour-left-
behind shape a third time. It is not: a test states plainly that WizeWorks is the
OPERATOR of both products and that the legal line names the operator, correct for
both. Reading it before filing it is the only reason this is a sentence rather
than an issue.

## Act 106 — Signed by the wrong company

Brandon read the invoice email act 105 had printed and asked whether a tenant's
invoice should be signed off by WizeWorks. **It should not, and I had already
looked at that exact line and cleared it.**

Act 105 saw `WizeWorks` under Juniper Row's bill and dismissed it on the strength
of a test comment reading "the operator, correct for both". "Both" means both
PRODUCTS, sparx and Piggles. Every case in that file renders `password-reset`,
whose reader is an owner who PAYS WizeWorks — where naming the operator is right.
The test never considered the other audience, and I read its comment instead of
its cases. [443].

**The platform had already decided this**, and implemented it in the other
renderer. `silica/frame.ts` — the frame every Builder email a shop sends goes
through — signs with the shop's name, the shop's address, and then "Sent with
<product>", small and last. Its own comment is the argument. That is [122], fixed
in act 9 of the P02 run; and [122] says the sweep before IT "fixed the React
templates. It missed the silica frame." Now it is the mirror image: the frame was
fixed and `_layout.tsx` was left behind. Three sweeps, three engines, each fixing
what the last one missed.

Four templates reach somebody who has never heard of us — the customer being
billed, a visitor who swapped an email address for a download, a visitor who
filled in a contact form, and a customer asked to sign a document. All four were
signed by the operating company. **Three of the four also carried our wordmark**,
because `header={false}` was a separate lever and exactly one template had ever
pulled it: the invoice took its masthead off with a long argument about how a
software product's name over somebody's bill reads, and its neighbours kept
theirs.

The signature request was the worst of them. It went to the signer on the
PLATFORM chassis and named no business at all — "Please review and sign / Hi
Tessa, your estimate EST-000041 is ready", our wordmark on top, our company at
the bottom, and a link asking a stranger to put their name to something. That is
the shape of a phishing attempt. It now names the shop in the heading, the body,
the subject and the footer; `signature-mail.ts` already loaded the document's
site, so it only had to pass the name.

The fix is ONE required prop. `audience: 'platform' | 'visitor'` decides the
masthead and the sign-off together, because they were never two questions, and
being required rather than defaulted is what stops the next visitor-facing
template being signed by us without anybody deciding to. It earned that
immediately: it caught a ninth call site (`renderAuthoredEmail`) no grep of the
templates folder would have found. That one turned out to be dead — broadcasts
render through the silica frame, which was already correct — but I only know that
because I checked before writing it down.

**Two tests were holding the defect in place.** `expect(out.html).toContain('WizeWorks')`
required the operator on EVERY template, which made it mandatory; it is now split
by audience. And "carries no platform masthead" had been asserting nothing at
all: React Email splits interpolated text with `<!-- -->` markers, so its
`indexOf` anchor returned -1 and `slice(0, -1)` handed it the whole document. It
passed only because the footer named a company rather than a product — the moment
the footer said "Sent with sparx", the masthead test failed for the wrong reason.
That file already records the last time an assertion of its own pinned a leak in
place. It has now done it twice.

## Act 107 — A new colorway, and the five shirts nobody had counted

Two defects. [172] had been open since act 3, parked as "Brandon's call". It was
not: the question in it has an obvious answer, and the answer is in the code
already. [444] is what proving the fix from the screen turned up.

### The code she typed, and the fourteen that ignored it

Devi typed `ASH-OVERSHIRT` into **Product code** when she added The Ash
Overshirt. Then she pressed **Give them all the same price**, and got fourteen
codes reading `THE-ASH-OVER-…` — built from the product's WEB ADDRESS, cut to
twelve characters, including the "The" she would never put on a label. One shirt,
two naming schemes, and the odd one out was the only one she wrote.

The generator read `product.handle`. It now reads the code the product already
carries — the version shown first, the same one the bulk fill already copies the
PRICE from — with that version's own choices taken back off the end, so anyone
who makes a generated version the default cannot compound
`ASH-OVERSHIRT-XS-CLAY-S-BONE`.

**The truncation was the second half, and it was not hypothetical.** Cutting the
stem to twelve characters is the only thing that let two products generate the
same code. Threadline has twelve products whose names all begin "Brushed Terry",
and every one of them falls to the stem `SAMPLE-BRUSH`; the second one filled in
would ask the server for a code the first already held and stop partway with
nothing to do about it. The stem is what makes a code unique across products, so
it is the last thing that may be shortened. **My own test caught me shortening it
anyway** — I fixed the stem source and left the web-address fallback still
slicing to twelve, which is the same defect one line down.

Proved as Devi, on her screen, with her data: added **Moss** (`#6E7B4F`) as a
fourth colorway for the autumn drop, pressed **Give them all the same price**,
and read the codes off the Variants tab. `ASH-OVERSHIRT-XS-MOSS` through
`ASH-OVERSHIRT-XL-MOSS`. Her scheme, extended.

### And five shirts went on sale that nobody had counted

The moment those five existed they were on her website, buyable, with no number
behind them — and their own setting says **Stop selling it** when they run out.
It never fires, because there is nothing to run out of. A version becomes
stock-managed by being COUNTED, not by existing, so an uncounted one sells
without limit. [444].

**That rule is right and deliberate**, and the code says so in capitals in two
places. Writing a zero row when a variant is created is the tempting fix and it
is a regression: it is how "nobody has counted this" became "there are none", and
how a bakery who typed in ten products watched her live shop tell every visitor
that all ten were sold out while she had bread on the counter. The storefront
selling `XS · Moss` was correct. **What was missing is that nobody told her.**

She went to look, the way she would. **Stock → Stock**, search `Ash Overshirt`:
**Showing 1-15 of 15**, on a shirt the products list says has twenty versions.
Searching the code itself: _"Nothing matches that."_ Both screens are about the
same shirt and they disagree by five, and neither says why.

The rule was right in one renderer and absent in two, which is the shape this run
keeps finding. The per-version card on **How many you have** has always said it
perfectly — "Not counted anywhere yet · Nobody has said how many of this version
you have, so your website sells it without limit" — but it is the twentieth card
down a pane, and the summary written so that "the question 'do I have any' is
settled before anyone reads a single row" did not mention it. Both silences are
the EMPTY case handled and the PARTIAL case missed: the pane has a message for a
product where nothing is counted, and the stock list asks the catalog when a
search returns nothing. Twelve counted and five not falls between them.

So: the pane's summary now names the mixed case, and the stock list carries a
band above the table saying what the table is not showing. The band needed a
question nobody could ask — the list starts `FROM inventory_levels`, so an
uncounted version is not a row it can return however it is filtered — which is
now `GET /v1/inventory/uncounted`. It is a band and not a filter chip on purpose:
a chip is something you have to know to press, and not knowing is the whole
defect.

**Sixty versions across fifteen businesses** are on sale, must be posted, say
"stop selling it when out", and have never been counted. They were invisible
everywhere until this.

Then the job, finished as Devi: **Count them** on the band opened the product's
stock pane, and six of each Moss size went in through **Record a count**. 78 to
sell became 108, the band disappeared, and her shop now shows `XS · Moss`
buyable with six behind it beside `XS · Bone` struck through at zero. That is the
first time on this platform a brand-new version has been counted in from the
screen that noticed it was missing.

Guards run red both ways. Dropping the stem fix reddens two of the six code
tests and leaves the truncation pair green; restoring the truncation reddens the
other two and leaves the stem pair green. On the query: removing the "no level
row" condition reddens four of five and leaves the draft/removed test green — the
green twin, since that one is about a different condition — and breaking the
on-sale condition reddens all five.

### Two more, noticed and not filed

Both were filed and fixed the same day, in act 108. Neither was a judgement call,
and writing them here rather than doing them was the mistake.

- **The count form defaults to the wrong place.** "Where you counted it" opens on
  **Fulfillment Center** every time, and every unit of stock Juniper Row has is
  at **Main Warehouse**. It is the first location alphabetically, and it does not
  remember the one just used, so counting five versions of one product means
  changing it five times and one slip puts stock in a building she does not use.
  **Filed as [445], fixed.**
- **Typing a hex into the color picker and pressing Escape discards it**, with no
  sign it was thrown away. Enter keeps it. Defensible, but the first attempt at
  Moss silently did nothing. **Not defensible on a second look: every slider in
  that panel commits live, so Escape is not a cancel and the HEX box was the odd
  one out. Filed as [447], fixed.**

## Act 108 — Ink, and the thirty-three things that did not need counting

Two loose ends from act 107, both of them things she had already put up with.
Closing the first one turned up a third, and the third was mine.

### The band was wrong about her shop, by thirty-three

`Stock → Stock`, cold, said **33 versions you sell have never been counted** and
named FRQ-BOOK-SIGNAL, FRQ-MEM-ANNUAL and FRQ-REP-AI26. Those are the Frequency
membership, a field guide and a report on AI infrastructure. Behind them, four
perfumes made to order, a discovery set, two totes and a sticker pack.

Every one of them is set to **keep selling when it runs out**. There was nothing
to count. The right number on that screen was **zero**.

This was a day-old regression of my own, and it is the plainest instance of "a
fix leaves its neighbour behind" I have written down: **the audit SQL in [444]
carries `AND v.inventory_policy = 'deny'` and the query that shipped does not.**
I measured with a filter I never implemented, then reported the measured number
as though the shipped code had produced it. The row payload even carries
`inventoryPolicy`, and 444's own text says why: _"the setting that is NOT being
honoured."_ Carried, documented, never read.

The size of it, asked of the database rather than guessed:

| what the version says | posted | uncounted, on sale |
| --------------------- | ------ | ------------------ |
| keep selling when out | yes    | **1,664**          |
| stop selling when out | yes    | **55**             |

Thirty times noise. And a band that names things which do not need doing is
worse than no band at all: the next time it says five, she has already learned
to skip it.

One rule now, `countingMatters`, asked by all three renderers and by the server:
"stop selling when out" cannot fire while nothing was ever counted, so that is a
broken promise and it is the news; "keep selling when out" is unlimited exactly
as asked and there is nothing to say; and a version that is never posted has no
shelf, so it cannot be counted at all. Filed and fixed as [446].

Her screen went **33 → nothing**, which is the correct answer for her shop.

### Where she counts things

"Where you counted it" opened on **Fulfillment Center** every time. Juniper Row
has 67 count rows in the **Main Warehouse** and **zero** at the Fulfillment
Center. It was `locations[0]` — the first location by name, because F comes
before M. Not a guess about her business; a fact about the alphabet.

The obvious answer was wrong. There IS a `defaultWarehouseId` on site settings,
and every one of the 35 rows on the platform carries a value — but nothing in the
console edits it, `bootstrapDefaults` writes it once by picking the oldest
warehouse, and **34 of the 35 have never been updated by anybody.** Treating a
machine-written guess as somebody's answer is how the wrong building gets
authority, so it reads evidence instead:

1. where this exact version is already counted;
2. where this person last recorded a count;
3. where the rest of this product is counted;
4. and only then the first place on the list.

Two above three deliberately. Somebody counting a delivery at the Fulfillment
Center must not be sent back to the Main Warehouse on the next version — the
place she picked ON PURPOSE has to survive. Filed and fixed as [445].

### Escape threw the color away

Typing a hex and pressing Escape lost it silently. Enter kept it, clicking away
kept it, only Escape discarded — because silica's HEX box holds a draft and
commits on blur, and Escape closes the popover without ever blurring.

Escape is not a cancel there and cannot be: every slider in the same panel
commits live, so closing already keeps them. The HEX box was the one control in
its own panel behaving differently, in the direction that loses work. And when
she is CHANGING a color rather than setting a first one, there is no signal at
all — the chip shows the old color, which looks exactly like a chip showing the
right one.

The chip is now one shared `SwatchPicker` that settles the field on the way out,
running the picker's own commit rather than re-implementing it. The upstream fix
is named at the top of the file so it can be deleted when silica lands it. Filed
and fixed as [447].

### The whole thing, proved as her

She added **Ink** to The Ash Overshirt for the autumn drop. Typed `#1F2A44` over
the default, pressed **Escape** — the chip went deep navy and the "No color
picked" warning cleared. **Change how it is sold** → **Give them all the same
price** → **Create them**.

Five new codes: `ASH-OVERSHIRT-XS-INK` and its siblings. Her product code, not
her web address, and not cut short — [172] still holding a day later.

The band came back reading **5 versions you sell have never been counted**, with
**Count them**, naming the Ink codes. Not 38. Five.

**Count them** opened the stock pane, and `ASH-OVERSHIRT-XS-INK` offered **Main
Warehouse** without being asked — rung 3, from its twenty counted siblings. Then
the test that matters in the other direction: with the Fulfillment Center
remembered, the next version opened on the **Fulfillment Center** despite all
twenty-one siblings being in the Main Warehouse. Her choice beat the evidence,
which is the whole point.

Then the actual work. Four, seven, five, six and three units of Ink, counted in a
row, **without touching the location picker once after the first**. 108 to sell
became 133. The band went away. Her shop shows `XS · Ink`, `S · Ink` and `M · Ink`
buyable beside `XS · Bone` struck through as sold out at a counted zero.

Every guard runs red on its own. Four separate breaks of the location ladder
redden four different single tests; breaking the policy filter reddens the
"keep selling" test and leaves the not-posted one green, and breaking the
shipping filter does the reverse.

### What this act is really about

Both of act 107's "noticed and not filed" notes were defects, and one of them was
hiding a bigger one. Neither was a judgement call. **Writing something down as
"noticed, not filed" is the same smell as `Blocked on: decision`** — a way of
recording work as though recording were the work.

## Open threads

Things noticed while doing something else and NOT chased, recorded so they are
not lost between sessions. None is filed as an issue, because none has been
verified as a defect.

- **"Set up payment" means two different things on one screen.** The trial
  banner says "Set up payment to keep your site online" and its button is about
  paying Piggles; four inches below it sits **How you take payment**, which is
  about her customers paying her. Nothing is broken and neither name is wrong on
  its own. Whether the banner should say something else is a judgement about the
  TRIAL banner, not about the payments pane, so it is recorded rather than filed.
- ~~**`businessName` on her public tenant payload reads "Saffron & Sage
  Catering"**~~ — **chased in act 95, filed as [431].** Not another tenant's
  name: no shop on the platform is called that, and it is one row, in her primary
  site's own brand override. It came from the catering BLUEPRINT. The guess in
  this note was wrong in a useful direction — chasing it found a live defect the
  note had not suspected (a design refresh could rename her business) and a
  second one behind it (no write to a site was audited at all).
- **Three of her seven sites still answer on `*.sparx.zone`** — archive, press,
  trade. Pre-fix rows from issue [316], all timestamped before it, and the code
  now mints `piggles.site` correctly. They are stale DATA, not a live defect, and
  there is no backfill for the ones already minted.
- ~~**`translations-data.ts` (361) and `translations-list.tsx` (276)** are over
  RULE #0.5~~ — **closed in act 86.** Both were edited there, so both were split
  along real boundaries rather than to hit a number: the pure locale + coverage
  functions moved to `translations-locale.ts` (97) and the row moved to
  `translation-row.tsx` (127), leaving 331 and 226. Everything still enters
  through `translations-data.ts`, which re-exports the moved names. The standing
  note about `content-type-detail.tsx` (1,808) is untouched.
- **Machine-speed typing artifacts, twice, both dismissed after re-testing.** A
  dropped character and a React "Maximum update depth exceeded" fired only when
  the automation typed ~55 characters into a controlled textarea in one
  synchronous burst; neither reproduced at human speed (single keystrokes, and
  chunked typing, both clean). Recorded rather than filed — a phantom defect
  costs more than an unrecorded one.
  Nothing must come back.

**Without a mouse.** The 15-variant grid, keyboard only. If it cannot be done,
that is the finding.

**Recorded for this run** — time from landing on meetpiggles to a live site,
how the lists feel at this business's volume, whether the growth board got its
contact + deal + `brand:piggles` tag, and whether the usage meters read sensibly
for this tenant.

| Wrong moves                  | **All four done, and every one produced defects.** _Refund the same Tee line twice:_ the RETURN door refuses correctly ("there is nothing left to add to a return"), the **Refund this order** door was broken twice over -- it offered $86.00 where the shop held $128.00 (subtracting refunds already given a second time), then refused with one of two different sentences depending on whether a cheque reference had been typed, each of them advising the manual refund the button had just offered to record. On a shop that takes cash or cheques it could never work at all, and the same mistake had already been found and fixed in the returns path under [223]. [303]. _Delete the Clay colorway with an open order against it:_ **the delete itself is handled well** -- named preview listing the exact codes, a confirm, a soft delete, both open orders still fulfilling, and the pick list it generated listed `THE-EVERYDAY-M-CLAY` as To pick. Putting the colorway BACK is what breaks: the five versions do not come back, the grid offers to create new ones at the codes they still hold, refuses them, and the bulk button silently writes `-CLAY-2` codes with **no stock** while 28 real garments sit on the retired rows. [305]. _Import the 25-customer CSV a second time:_ nothing duplicated, which is the half that already worked — but the run reported **"25 of 25 brought over"**, word for word what twenty five ARRIVALS produce, when the answer was none new and twenty five overwritten, and the overwrite reached the marketing opt-in: a man who had asked to come off the newsletter went back on it. [309](issues/309-she-re-imported-her-list-and-it-put-someone-who-unsubscribed-back-on-it.md). Checking how that opt-in is read then found [310](issues/310-the-people-who-never-confirmed-came-in-as-people-she-can-email.md): a Mailchimp export’s unconfirmed subscribers land contactable, because the adapter writes "not confirmed" as an empty string and the row builder drops those. _A sale code on an order that gets undone:_ the console rightly offers no way to discount a placed order, so the move is the consequence. Cancelling an unpaid order left the customer’s one use of SPRING15 spent for good — **"You’ve already used this discount the maximum number of times"**, about an order that no longer exists on a sale running another month — while her basket went on carrying the same code at −$20.70 with a live checkout button. Refused when she asked, served when she did not. [312](issues/312-she-cancelled-the-order-and-took-the-sale-away-from-the-customer-for-good.md). |
| Reload · deep link · restore | Deep link: **yes**. Her own product and her own order both open from a pasted address; a record that is not hers refuses and now says so ([286], [287]). Reload: partly -- reloaded onboarding after the failure and the name came back from the tenant while the trade and the ticks did not, the reverse of what was saved ([163]). Restore: **yes** — closed the Site layout pane, its tab vanished and the strip re-flowed; opened **Recent** in the rail, which listed it; clicking it brought the pane back showing the Archive site's header, with `site.account-link` still in its layer tree and "Saved and live". The whole eleven-pane arrangement also restores on reload, on every navigation this session. A reopened pane lands at the END of the strip rather than where it was closed from; noted, not filed, because reopening and re-placing are different promises and only the first is made.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Dates                        | **Done — both halves driven, and the second half was broken.** Machine is UTC−7 (PDT). Devi's console shows SPRING15's stored `2026-10-01 06:59 UTC` as **09/30/2026 11:59 PM**, correct in her own time. **The last hour works**: sale ending 00:04, code applied at 23:21:38 for −$20.70. **The first hour after did not refuse it.** At 23:22, an hour past the end, the basket still showed the code, every checkout step showed the saving, and the button placed **O-000011 at $126.30 against a sale that ended at 22:21** — while Devi's own Discounts list correctly read **Ended**. [300], major: the window was checked only when the code was TYPED. Proving it turned up [301] as well — a code typed after checkout began never reached the total, because the session froze the money at `start()` and nothing refreshed it. Both fixed together: the basket sheds a lapsed saving on read and on every mutation, an in-flight session is kept in line with its basket, the order is priced from the settled basket, and the button's own total is sent back so nothing can be written for a figure she was never shown. Re-run: the same press now answers _"The total changed while you were checking out. Open your basket to see what it comes to now."_ and writes nothing.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Money edge                   | **Done, and it produced the run's worst defect.** Built the named case — a discounted order on the free-shipping threshold — as Anneliese: $110 trouser + $42 tee = $152.00, over her $150 line so shipping was free, then SPRING15 for −$22.80. Cart, all three checkout steps and the button itself said **$129.20**. The order written said **$152.00, discount 0.00**, and so did her order history and Devi's console, which showed no discount line at all and no sign a code had been used. The code was still spent: a $22.80 redemption row, usage count 2, `per_customer_limit` 1. [298], blocker. `computeTotals` had no discount parameter while `CreateOrderInput` declared one — accepted, validated, never read, with `taxTotal` beside it carrying exactly the override discount was missing. Two callers believed in it, checkout and the orders CSV import. Fixed at the spine plus a per-line apportionment so a refund has the right basis, and re-proved as Jo at $138.00: button $126.30, order 138.00 / −20.70 / +9.00 / **126.30**, lines $14.40 and $6.30 off. **On the threshold question itself: free shipping reads the PRE-discount subtotal.** Anneliese shipped free while paying $129.20; Jo's $138.00 was charged $9.00. Consistent, and both readings of "free over $150" are defensible, so recorded rather than filed — Devi has never been asked which she means.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Buyer's side                 | **Done, and it was the richest hour of the run — six defects.** Anneliese had no login, so she made one; the signup matched her existing customer row rather than duplicating it, and her order was there. Everything around it was wrong: the shop said **Sign in** to her on every page and gave her no route to her account ([291]); her order showed the full $170 and "Payment Partially Paid" without ever mentioning the $42 refunded ([292]); the status card drew three black rules across itself ([293]); the order list rendered as a centered stack ([294]); one screen called the same event `fulfilled`, `Fulfilled` and `Shipped` ([295]). Fixing [291] then exposed [296] — the whole upgrade-on-read that heals stale chrome had never been called by anything. **Self-service returns did not exist** on the storefront -- the only path was Requests -> "Ask for help", which is the three messages back and forth Devi came here to stop. Brandon called it into scope, and it is now built and seeded ([297]): a customer picks lines, a reason and refund-or-swap from the order itself, both sides can follow it, and the shop is told. That closes her third goal.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Someone else's business      | **Done, and nothing leaked.** Searched `Thistle`, `sourdough` and `Lindqvist` -- "Nothing in your orders, customers or products matches" each time. Deep-linked Thistle & Rye's product and order ids: both refused, 404, RLS re-checked as the app role (0 rows). The site switcher offers only Juniper Row. Two defects in what the refusal SAID -- [286], [287].                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| One job without a mouse      | **Done, and it produced [319].** The job itself finished end to end: ⌘K → `Marlow` → Enter opened the product; Tab to the pane tabs, Right Right to Variants (focus moved, the panel did not, which is the correct manual-activation pattern), Enter; Tab to **XS · Oat**, which took a clear ring around the whole row; Enter expanded it; Tab into Price; `102.00`; Shift+Tab to Save; Enter. Every unsaved signal fired together — row badge, "1 version has unsaved changes", a dot on the Variants tab AND on the pane tab, the status-strip line, Save going live — and every one cleared on save; the database reads `10200`. What was broken is the distance: **62 Tab presses** to reach the pane at all, ~15 through the rail and three per open pane through the tab strip, thirty of them close buttons, with no skip control and F6 doing nothing. Fixed with a skip control that appears at Tab 2. It jumps the rail and **not** the tab strip, because landing past the strip means knowing which pane is active and that is dockview's to say — recorded that way in the code and the issue rather than faked. _Also observed, not proven:_ Ctrl+S does not save, it typed an `s` into the price field which the field sanitized away on blur; may be an artifact of how the automation delivers modifiers.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Time to live site            | Not measurable yet — the site is not built. Started 02:53 PDT; act 3 opened 04:47 PDT. That elapsed figure measures the repairs, not the product, so it is not offered as one.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| Speed at real volume         | **Fine, and the check itself found two defects.** Products (16 products / 60 versions) and Customers (34 on the primary site, 30 on Archive) both paint instantly — no spinner long enough to read, no pagination needed at 50 per page, and the filter chips respond without a pause. At her real volume the lists are not the problem. What the check DID find is that the Customers list opened on the wrong people: sorted by **Recent order**, every one of her buyers was BELOW every lead who had never ordered, because Postgres sorts `DESC` nulls-first ([322]). Following the buyers it surfaced led to [323]. So: fast, and until today pointed at the wrong end of her list.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Growth board                 | **Done, and this is where P03 differs from P01.** P01 recorded it as `not checked` because there was no system tenant to write to; there is one now, and Devi's signup landed properly. Under the `wizeworks` tenant: contact `960fe725` (`p03.devi@piggles.test`), deal `d708cb12` (`Juniper Row (juniper-row)`), source `signup`, stage Trial, trial ending Sep 6, acquisition `marketing-site` / `pricing-hero`, and her Piggles rail answer `[web, sell, people]` carried through. The tag is **`brand-piggles`**, not `brand:piggles` as this file and the other nine persona files say — `toTag` rewrites the colon because the tag column rejects one, so the docs are the stale half, not the code. The **story fields were all null**, for every Piggles tenant AND every sparx tenant in the database, which is [320] — fixed, and the trade now reaches the board.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Usage rollup                 | **Done — reads sensibly, and handles what it has not measured correctly.** On getpiggles › Your account, _What you are using_: **People on your team 1**, **Websites 5** (Juniper Row, Archive, Journal, Press, Trade — exactly her five), **Locations 2**, and then **Customers**, **Photos and files** and **Messages sent this month** all reading **"Not measured yet"** above the line _"Your first overnight count has not run yet, so some of these are still blank."_ That is the right answer rather than a missing one: `/v1/usage/capacity` preserves `null` deliberately — its own comment says a zero "would be read as a measurement" — and the screen says so in her words. **Two counts worth knowing rather than filing:** "Locations 2" counts STOCK locations, and both of Devi's (`Main Warehouse`, `Fulfillment Center`) were created by setup rather than by her — a one-person studio with no shop was given a fulfilment centre it did not ask for. And no meter shows a ceiling or an over state, which matches the page saying plainly that "adding a payment method, seeing invoices, and buying more room are the next thing being built". **Also confirmed [316] from the owner's side here:** _Your business address_ reads **`juniper-row.piggles.site`**.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |

| [288](issues/288-her-customers-history-named-his-order-by-a-string-of-gibberish.md)                               | minor    | Her customer's history named his order by a string of gibberish                                          | yes                               | Rang up a real counter sale for Ravi: his feed now reads "Order O-000008 placed ($42.00)", above the Aug 24 line still showing its id                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

| [285](issues/285-every-basket-said-the-shopper-had-been-there-at-4-07.md)                                         | major    | Every basket said the shopper had been there at 4:07                                                     | fixed                             | `markAbandoned` stamped `updated_at`, which the console renders as "Last active" — the sweep flattened the column that ranks the queue                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
